Patriots activate Mack Hollins from IR

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The Patriots won’t have A.J. Brown or Romeo Doubs on Sunday, but they will be getting another wide receiver back from injury against the Packers.

They have activated Mack Hollins from injured reserve. Hollins returned to practice this week after missing the last four games with a calf injury.

Hollins had 15 catches for 209 yards in four appearances before his injury. He had 46 catches for 550 yards and two touchdowns in his first season with the Patriots.

Brown will miss his second straight game with a knee injury and Doubs remains on injured reserve. Pop Douglas, Efton Chism, and Kyle Williams join Hollins as the available receivers on the 53-man roster for New England.

Why Does The Cat In The Hat Have A Flip Phone? Bill Hader Has Thoughts

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Carmen Electra Went Full Catwoman For Halloween (But I Didn't See Kat Dennings As A Carrot Coming)

I Wondered Why Nathan Fielder Was Picked For You Can See Everything. His Answer Confused Me

I Just Found Out What Christopher Nolan Made Universal’s Head Honcho Do When He Pitched The Odyssey

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

One thing I love about book-to-screen adaptations of classics like The Cat In The Hat is how each new iteration reimagines a character we already love. Bill Hader’s version comes over two decades after the Mike Myers movie, and one peculiar new thing I noticed about his animated take on the character is the fact that he has a flip phone. Random, right?

When CinemaBlend spoke to Hader about his latest role, I had to ask about the little detail I noticed about his The Cat In The Hat. Here’s how the SNL alum responded:

Because Cat in the Hat's real. Cat in the Hat doesn't need to fit in with whatever's happening now, you know?

For some reason, it just feels right that The Cat would have a flip phone (and one that even has a crystal-clear video chat function). Plus, this detail about The Cat definitely adds to his unique character in a cute way, as it's revealed in the film that he’s been doing house calls to bring play into the lives of kids for multiple generations. So, perhaps the flip phone tells us that he's a bit old school. Bill Hader also said this about the topic:

I think it was funny that it was a flip phone. I have friends who have flip phones, and they remember things better than me. They are not as distracted as me. They seem much happier than me.

Hey, maybe The Cat is onto something. I mean the character is all about bringing playtime and wonder to kids. Why would he need a smartphone for that?

These days, a lot more people are ditching their smartphones for more primitive technology like flip phones to get away from things like social media addiction. Chris Pine, for example, had one for four years before switching to the latest technology and finding himself wanting to “immediately go back”. Other famous people known to have a flip phone are Christopher Nolan, Anna Wintour and Rihanna – so The Cat can count himself among icons.

A lot of other Y2K trends have been coming back lately, too. For example, both Kylie Jenner and Ariana Grande recently admitted their love for wired earphones (and I’m totally with them). I’ve seen a lot of younger people out with their digital cameras at concerts too. When you think about it, The Cat In The Hat’s “dumb phone” is very in with the kids.

There’s already The Cat In The Hat trend; maybe this could be part of a new wave of less phone dependence and more focus on fun and creativity, like the character itself has been teaching kids since the picture book came out back in 1957.

You can check out the new The Cat In The Hat movie in theaters now.

Now she's into covering YA television and movies, and plenty of horror. Word webslinger. All her writing should be read in Sarah Connor’s Terminator 2 voice over.

Path of Exile 2 Shadow class preview: Rogues do it from behind!

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

What's better than killing your enemies before they even know what happened?

Destructoid is at ExileCon 2026 all the way in Auckland, New Zealand, checking out what awaits us in Path of Exile 2‘s fabled 1.0 release as well as what’s new for the first game.

And the first of those relates to the new Shadow class—a stealthy rogue sneaking up on entire groups of enemies and taking them out before their first strike lands. I played it for about 15 minutes yesterday, experiencing several of its exceptionally complex and difficult-to-master abilities, and using them to take on large mob waves in less-than-spectacular fashion. But that is exactly the thing with the Shadow: even if you do not know what you’re doing, the class is pure eye candy, and everything that you succeed in makes it seem like you’re some kind of pro.

The Shadow specializes in stealth and can turn invisible on a whim. This invisibility breaks when you attack or when you get too close to an enemy or if the enemies discover your shadow clone and destroy it.

While invisible, you can cast traps, setting and piling them up on a large group of enemies and ensuring your victory before the battle even happens. The traps come in many different forms: poison gases, concussion grenades, and so on, and each interacts with others in cool and unique ways. For example, casting a poison gas trap and combining it with an explosive trap results in a massive burst of flame that ignites enemies and deals a ton of fire damage. Concussion grenades can further disperse these flames and gas, significantly increasing AOE.

However, being the absolute bot that I am, I went for a one-button run, using only the daggers and the class’s shadow clone jutsu to deal incredible damage in a large AOE using nothing but my stabs since, well, the shadows aren’t just there for show. They repeat most attacks that you do yourself, meaning that you can create a dozen of them, all of which will lambast your target with the same dagger attack that you yourself used. And they do it in a way that’d put League of Legends‘ Zed to shame, and Naruto couldn’t even hold a candle to them.

The Shadow is thus all about setting up the attack and executing it with lightning speed. You create a bunch of clones and go all-in on your enemy together with them, lay a bunch of traps using stealth, or both.

Of course, since I had a very limited time with the class, I couldn’t really learn the ropes in time and maximize its capabilities, but I’m pretty sure this could turn out to be the most powerful class in the entire game. The strength, naturally, comes from its complexity, meaning the best players are going to find wild ways of utilizing the Shadow to decimate even the most challenging enemies and bosses in basically single strikes.

What I really loved about it overall is that it finally brings the Rogue fantasy into PoE2. We can be proper assassins and thieves (called infiltrators here) and do what rogues do best: kill our foes before they ever know what’s going on.

The Shadow drops together with Path of Exile 2‘s 1.0 update on Dec. 11.

Forget ‘Squid Game,’ Netflix’s Brutal Psychological Thriller With Only 16 Episodes Is Even Better

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Amanda M. Castro is a Network TV writer at Collider and a New York–based journalist whose work has appeared in Newsweek, where she is an Associate Editor, and The U.S. Sun, where she previously served as a Senior Consumer Reporter.

She specializes in network television coverage, delivering sharp, thoughtful analysis of long-running procedural hits and ambitious new dramas across broadcast TV. At Collider, Amanda explores character arcs, storytelling trends, and the cultural impact of network series that keep audiences tuning in week after week.

Born and raised in Puerto Rico, Amanda is bilingual and holds a degree in Communication, Film, and Media Studies from the University of New Haven.

The Glory is a strong contender for this title because it gives you a more cohesive form of brutality, an intimately violent experience compared to that of Squid Game. In 16 episodes, it tells the story of one girl’s meticulously calculated revenge against classmates who wronged her.

The series is a tough watch at times. Its opening episodes show the damage that school bullying can leave behind, and the story doesn’t treat that violence as a passing teenage cruelty. But The Glory turns that painful setup into an intricate revenge drama, anchored by Song Hye-kyo’s controlled, quietly ferocious performance.

In her teenage years, Moon Dong-eun is harassed by a group of affluent peers directed by Park Yeon-jin. Their bullying is systematic, and when Dong-eun tries to seek help, both the school and authorities let her down. Years later, Dong-eun has rebuilt her life around one goal: making the people who hurt her answer for what they did.

Now an adult, Dong-eun becomes a teacher at the elementary school Yeon-jin’s daughter attends. She has spent years preparing, and her return puts her close to the former ringleader and the rest of the group. Her plan doesn’t rely on one dramatic confrontation. She studies her targets, finds their weak spots, and nudges them toward turning on one another. Each new piece of the plan shifts the balance between Dong-eun and the people who once had all the power.

Song Hye-kyoo shows restraint as Dong-eun, saying more with a few gestures. She remains serious and hard to read, and the show suggests Dong-eun's calm stems from deep past suffering. The scars of Dong-eun from her past trauma are shown at various times; at times, through things like a small sound provoking a painful memory or so on.

Lim Ji-yeon’s portrayal of Yeon-jin makes her a formidable adversary, however. In her adult life, she is successful in her profession and should be happy with her family, but she still has the same sense of entitlement. Dong-eun has waited a long time for her once-in-a-lifetime confrontation, while Yeon-jin has lived under the false assumption that her past will not return.

The supporting cast gives the revenge plot room to deepen. Lee Do-hyun plays Joo Yeo-jeong, a doctor who supports Dong-eun and carries his own sorrow, and Yeom Hye-ran plays Kang Hyeon-nam, a character who alternates between helping Dong-eun and trying to escape her own troubled life. Their narratives ensure revenge isn't just a list of people getting punished.

The series’ two-part release gives the story space to build before the payback takes hold. The first half lays out Dong-eun’s preparation and the web of relationships around her targets. The second turns those connections into pressure points. It’s satisfying to watch pieces fall into place, but the plan stays messy enough to keep the tension alive.

That’s where The Glory differs from Squid Game. While both series are successful Netflix shows that reveal characters trapped in harsh systems, The Glory narrows its focus and directly confronts bullying, privilege, and failing institutions instead of the game itself. The show also resists making revenge feel simple. Dong-eun’s methods can be manipulative and morally fraught, and the series leaves room to sit with that discomfort. You can root for her to expose the people who escaped accountability while still wondering what she’ll have left when the plan is over.

The early abuse scenes are intense, so viewers who find depictions of bullying and violence difficult should know what they’re stepping into. But The Glory doesn’t use those moments as cheap shock. They detail the hardships Dong-eun faces throughout her formative years and why she still suffers from them as an adult.

‘The American Dream Has an Address’—and Both Parties Are on Notice, Strategists Say

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Democrats' historic midterm sweep will hand them control of Congress, but keeping it could depend on whether they can make housing more affordable, according to leading strategists from both parties.

"The American dream has an address," veteran Democratic strategist Donna Brazile told a room of real estate professionals in New Orleans on Saturday.

Speaking alongside conservative political strategist Shermichael Singleton at the National Association of Realtors®' NAR NXT conference, Brazile cautioned that Democrats' electoral victories were not an unconditional endorsement of their policies, but a demand for relief from the rising cost of living.

Singleton agreed, arguing that Republicans had already learned that lesson the hard way.

"Our voters felt we fundamentally failed," he said, pointing to rising fuel and food costs as well as younger Americans' inability to afford homes as examples of the administration's unfulfilled affordability promises.

Brazile warned that Democrats could face the same reckoning in 2028 if they fail to act.

"If you're not going to help build more houses and take care of the affairs of the American people, they will kick our [expletive] in two years," she said.

The affordability crisis that helped propel Democrats to victory Tuesday had been a liability for their own party just two years earlier—a fact that Brazile said is a warning to both parties heading into 2028.

"Affordability has been on the minds of American people now for two consecutive years," Brazile said, acknowledging that Joe Biden and Kamala Harris had failed to recognize the depth of voters' frustration ahead of the 2024 election.

And today, housing offers perhaps the clearest illustration of why that frustration has persisted.

Mortgage rates climbed from roughly 3% in 2021 to nearly 8% by late 2023, compounding a pandemic-era surge in home prices, Lawrence Yun, chief economist at the National Association of Realtors, explained during a separate presentation Saturday.

And while borrowing costs have since eased, home prices continue to climb. Prices rose in approximately 78% of U.S. metropolitan markets during the third quarter of 2026, according to NAR's latest quarterly report.

Existing homeowners have benefited from that appreciation, with Yun estimating that the typical owner has gained nearly $200,000 in housing wealth since before the pandemic. Prospective buyers, meanwhile, face a considerably higher cost of entry.

The consequences are particularly stark for younger Americans. First-time buyers now account for just 22% of home purchases, while their median age has reached 40, matching last year's record high, according to NAR's newly released 2026 Profile of Home Buyers and Sellers.

"Maybe the election result was partly reflecting that fact," Yun said, pointing to younger Americans struggling to enter the housing market.

For Singleton, 36, the frustration is personal.

"You do everything right. You get the college degree. You get the graduate degree. You start the family. You can't afford anything," he said.

Singleton purchased his first home at 28, but said many of his friends, now approaching or entering their 40s, still can’t afford to buy.

And with millennials and Gen Z now accounting for a substantial share of the electorate, Singleton argued that housing access could become a more decisive factor in how younger Americans vote.

Amid deep political divisions, Brazile and Singleton's agreement on the urgency of the housing crisis was striking. And already, there's a powerful precedent for cooperation on the issue.

The 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, enacted in July, received overwhelming support from both parties, passing the Senate 85-5 and the House 358-32. The legislation aims to boost supply by reducing regulatory barriers, expanding financing opportunities, and supporting new approaches to construction.

But passage was only the first step. Many provisions still require implementation or funding, and their effects on the market could take years to materialize.

Singleton sees opportunities to build on that foundation by addressing barriers closer to home, including local permitting delays, construction costs, and tariffs on Canadian lumber.

"One of the things that I hear about often from them is that the length of time it takes to get something approved is very costly," he said, referring to conversations with builders.

He also proposed investing in training younger Americans as electricians, plumbers, and other skilled tradespeople to help build the millions of homes the country needs.

It's an idea echoed by Scott Norman, CEO of the Texas Association of Builders, who identified workforce development as a bipartisan priority in an exclusive interview with Realtor.com following Democrats' sweep of the state's elected offices.

"The more folks we can get in the business, that'll drive down those labor costs," Norman said.

Brazile, meanwhile, said Democrats' affordability agenda places housing alongside groceries, energy, and family expenses, with an emphasis on cutting red tape and expanding supply.

"Affordability, affordability, affordability. It's not a slogan that Democrats can use again unless they do something about it," she said.

But delivering on those promises will require something neither party has consistently demonstrated in recent years: a willingness to compromise.

"We have the money, but we need the will," Brazile said.

In an emotional moment of the one-hour session, Brazile recalled working with Republican President George W. Bush to secure federal assistance after Hurricane Katrina devastated the city in 2005.

Despite their political differences, Brazile said she worked with the administration to secure funding for housing recovery, infrastructure, and a new veterans' medical center.

"That's bipartisanship. That's compromise. That's what it looks like," she said.

Her closing message to the real estate agents in attendance was to bring that same commitment to the newly elected lawmakers who will shape housing policy.

"You know the stories because you live it every day," she told them, urging agents to share the experiences of families struggling to afford homes.

And with a new Congress preparing to take office, Brazile argued, that firsthand knowledge could help turn promises of affordability into policies that deliver.

Allaire Conte is a senior advice writer covering real estate and personal finance trends. She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

He Played With Gerrard and Owen at Liverpool - Now He’s Serving a 1-Year Sentence

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

It's a fall from grace to go from playing alongside Steven Gerrard, Michael Owen, Nicolas Anelka and even Jay-Jay Okocha to facing legal troubles.

Premier League football provides access to serious money and fame, which doesn't always lead people down the right path in life.

The Brazilian has been behind bars for over two years now.

Countless footballers have ended up on the wrong side of the law. Adam Johnson, Joey Barton and Duncan Ferguson are just some of the ex-Premier League players who ended up behind bars for various reasons.

Gerrard is widely regarded as one of the greatest midfielders in Premier League history.

But he isn't held in such high regard by every teammate he ever came across. Gerrard famously didn't get along with ex-Liverpool colleague El-Hadji Diouf.

Eric Cantona, Diego Maradona and Mario Balotelli are all included in the list of 11 most controversial footballers of all-time

The duo have publicly criticised each other several times, including when Diouf attacked Gerrard so harshly on live TV that Emmanuel Petit had to step in to defend Gerrard.

The former Senegal forward was a controversial figure throughout his time on the pitch. He's now found himself in hot water after retiring in 2015.

Sadio Mane is one of the best players Liverpool have had in the past 20 years - but he grew up idolising a controversial ex-Reds ace.

Diouf was sued by his ex-wife Valerie Bishop after failing to pay just under £14,000, according to the claim. The retired footballer was ordered to pay £670 a month in child support payments when Valerie took custody of their daughter following their 2023 divorce.

He was also supposed to pay for her school fees and medical bills, but his ex-partner claimed he had failed to do so since 2024. A court ordered Diouf to repay £13,220 and​​​​​​​ handed a suspended one-year jail term.

Diouf arrived at Anfield with a big reputation following his impressive 2002 World Cup campaign with Senegal. He even persuaded the Reds to sign him instead of Nicolas Anelka, a decision Gerrard has since criticised.

The Senegalese forward lasted just two seasons at Anfield before moving on to Bolton Wanderers, on loan at first and then permanently. During his short-lived Liverpool career, Diouf scored just six goals in 80 appearances, 66 of which came alongside Gerrard.

Tensions were reportedly high between the two behind the scenes, and it even got physical once. Former Liverpool ace Florent Sinama-Pongolle recalled a clash between Gerrard and Diouf that left him 'traumatised'.

He told talkSPORT: "It was half-time of a pre-season game. There was a fight between Diouf and Gerrard. I was traumatised.

"Can you imagine the young ones seeing this and thinking that’s what professionals are like at that level?

"At half-time, in the dressing room. Stevie G is all like ‘you have to pass, you have to pass’ and [Diouf] just loses it."

Sinama-Pongolle continued, explaining that Diouf got extremely personal towards his teammate. He explained:

"He didn’t speak English. His English was rubbish. You know what he did? They hated each other so much. Steven Gerrard arrives; he insults Diouf, saying: 'Hey, you f*****'.

"And [Diouf] couldn’t answer, so he grabs Gerard Houllier and says: 'Tell him, I’ll f*** his mum.’ He came in and said: ‘I’m not his mate, I’ll do him in straight away.'"

5 Incredible Anime Arcs That Are 10/10, No Notes

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

These are some of the very best arcs in the history of anime

An exceptional story arc can elevate even a flawed anime series. Although the mechanics of a great arc vary, the best tend to raise the stakes, test their characters, and build steadily toward a cathartic payoff. The conflict must also feel convincing, with the antagonist’s evolving goals and the heroes’ shifting motivations driving the story forward.

Consider the Chimera Ant arc from Hunter x Hunter, which is nothing short of a rite of passage. It pushes the anime’s deconstruction of shonen conventions to its limits while sending Gon Freecss and Killua Zoldyck down starkly different paths. The Shibuya Incident arc from Jujutsu Kaisen is similarly transformative, shattering the established order of jujutsu society through its intersecting perspectives, major character deaths, and relentless succession of battles.

Perfection is subjective, but the finest anime arcs share a few qualities: They’re memorable, well-paced, emotionally compelling, and resolved in a meaningful way. With those standards in mind, these five story arcs come remarkably close to perfection. We wouldn’t change a thing.

This article contains major spoilers for every story arc discussed below.

What happens when a brilliant story ends with an equally brilliant final arc?

Fullmetal Alchemist: Brotherhood is filled with memorable stretches, from its exploration of the Ishvalan War to the heroes’ hunt for the Homunculi. But the anime saves its best for last with the Promised Day arc, a chaotic culmination of Father’s 400-year plan for Amestris and the intertwined destinies of Edward and Alphonse Elric.

Greed’s rebellion becomes a decisive turning point, while the brothers’ journey ultimately returns them to the Gate of Truth for one final reckoning.

The intricate buildup allows this concluding stretch to operate on an enormous scale. State Alchemists join forces with military defectors, Xingese royalty, and ordinary citizens to form a resistance against Father. The full complexity of Amestrian politics finally comes into play as nearly every faction and character converges upon Central.

Yet the arc never loses sight of the deeply personal story at its center. Amid the battles and political upheaval, the focus remains on Edward and everything he has learned about sacrifice, equivalence, and human connection. The Promised Day arc displays all the strengths of a thoughtful, well-constructed shonen, balancing quieter character moments with spectacular battles without allowing either to overwhelm the other.

Edward’s final sacrifice feels inevitable and surprising at once. His decision completes his development without robbing him of the life he fought so hard to reclaim — a fitting conclusion for someone with a Fullmetal heart.

One could argue that the Berserk: The Golden Age Arc film trilogy, which adapts the corresponding portion of Kentaro Miura’s Berserk, isn’t technically perfect. Despite its stunning hand-drawn artwork, the blend of 2D and 3D animation can feel uneven.

Even so, the trilogy captures the essence of the Golden Age, a breathtaking exploration of ambition, psychological trauma, betrayal, and the tenuous relationships that give people hope. All three films — The Egg of the King, The Battle for Doldrey, and The Advent — carefully portray the deteriorating bond between Guts, Griffith, and Casca, along with how each character responds to grief, desire, and loss.

Griffith and Guts are established as foils from the beginning, but their relationship develops with extraordinary depth and nuance. Guts, shaped by a brutal childhood and hardened by the need to survive, gradually lowers his defenses and learns to trust others. Griffith’s fall from a magnetic leader into a man consumed by pain and ambition destroys that progress, plunging Guts back into a cycle of anguish and revenge.

The Eclipse provides the arc’s unforgettable climax and establishes the horror that will define Guts’ subsequent campaign against the Apostles and the God Hand. Hope and despair are crushed together throughout the Golden Age, forcing its characters to confront the terrible limits of human resilience. It is unforgiving, devastating, and almost impossible to forget.

Mob Psycho 100 is pure shonen excellence. It uses the genre’s familiar framework to tell a radically subversive coming-of-age story about empathy, self-acceptance, and human connection.

Shigeo “Mob” Kageyama spends most of the series trying to control his immense psychic powers. But the emphasis isn’t on physical training or becoming stronger. Mob’s real challenge is developing the emotional intelligence necessary to understand himself and connect honestly with the people around him.

Because Mob’s powers are inextricably linked to his psychological state, the ???% arc serves as the culmination of years of emotional suppression. It answers the question of what happens when an overpowered protagonist finally reaches his breaking point. The ultimate conflict isn’t between Mob and another supernaturally gifted antagonist. It is between Mob and the part of himself he has always refused to accept.

His repressed emotions and unresolved trauma manifest as ???%, also known as the “Unknown.” The resulting conflict cannot be resolved through a conventional power-up or some last-minute supernatural solution. Instead, Reigen Arataka’s unflinching honesty finally allows Mob to confront the truth. Mob accepts the Unknown not as a separate monster that must be defeated, but as an intrinsic part of himself.

Mob Psycho 100 resolves its protagonist’s deepest conflict without offering an easy way out. Mob still has to live with his trauma and continue the difficult work of remaining emotionally open. But judging by his carefree laughter at the end of the anime, he is finally ready to do that work.

A sprawling anime like Naruto is bound to contain more than one standout arc, especially when its central story is interspersed with so many filler episodes. The Chunin Exams and Sasuke Retrieval arcs remain popular for good reason, but the most viscerally uncompromising portion of the series is Pain’s Assault.

Nagato, or Pain, is among the saga’s most compelling antagonists. His attack forces the Hidden Leaf Village to contend with total destruction while compelling Naruto and his allies to confront the ideology behind his violence.

Jiraiya’s death during his infiltration of Amegakure casts a shadow over everything that follows. But Nagato’s motivations extend beyond simple revenge. He believes humanity can achieve peace only after experiencing suffering so catastrophic that people become terrified of repeating it. Because empathy cannot be imposed, Nagato instead attempts to manufacture it through devastation, convincing himself that this atrocity is justified in the pursuit of lasting peace.

Naruto explores that philosophy through both dialogue and combat, using the visual language of a sprawling, devastating battle to deliver the arc’s greatest emotional and dramatic highs. For a time, no character feels safe, and the apparent destruction of the Hidden Leaf fundamentally destabilizes the series’ familiar world.

That sense of danger doesn’t truly lift until Naruto rejects the endless cycle of retaliation. Rather than defeating Nagato through force alone, he chooses empathy, resolving their ideological conflict by demonstrating the very compassion that Nagato believed could exist only through shared suffering.

The Steel Ball Run anime is now taking viewers through the early stages of its cross-country race, but it has not yet reached the Civil War arc. For now, this brilliant segment of JoJo’s Bizarre Adventure: Steel Ball Run remains a manga story waiting to make the leap to animation.

Hirohiko Araki excels at transforming abstract concepts into bizarre, visually inventive conflicts. The entirety of JoJo’s Bizarre Adventure is proof of his profound eccentricity, but the Civil War arc stands out even within a series built upon surreal confrontations.

The arc’s titular Stand belongs to government agent Axl RO and transforms the guilt carried by Johnny Joestar, Hot Pants, and Gyro Zeppeli into physical manifestations. Johnny’s accumulated trauma is laid bare, exposing the defining childhood experiences that continue to haunt him.

These manifestations can assume virtually any form, from a broken knife to a teddy bear. Once touched, they surround their target in a membrane that slowly crushes them to death. The only way to endure this nightmare is to confront the guilt that gave it form.

That forces Johnny to reckon with his past and unlock a new stage of his Spin ability. Jesus Christ himself also appears during the conflict, reinforcing a story already steeped in ideas about faith, personal transformation, and the destructive consequences of unchecked ambition.

The Sugar Mountain and High Voltage/D4C arcs are exceptional in their own right, but Civil War represents a monumental turning point for Johnny. It transforms an inventive Stand battle into something resembling a spiritual rebirth — and proves that even by JoJo standards, Araki is capable of getting profoundly weird.

Thank you for subscribing to our newsletter,

DC’s Upcoming Green Lantern Release Is Everything The DCU’s Next Project Should Be

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

DC reveals the first glimpse at Hal Jordan's next battle, which looks like the ideal basis for the DCU's future Green Lantern movie and show appearances. HBO’s Lanterns marks a remarkable milestone as the first live-action, non-cameo Green Lantern project since the critical and commercial stumble of 2011's Green Lantern movie. The fifteen-year drought feels especially strange given how central the Emerald Knights are to the broader DC Universe and the superhero genre as a whole. Yet, for over a decade following Ryan Reynolds’s polarizing outing, the Lanterns were largely relegated to brief, peripheral nods.

Fortunately, this return is only the beginning, as the Green Lanterns are playing a vital role in the expanding DCU. Following the events of the HBO series' first season, both Aaron Pierre’s John Stewart and Nathan Fillion’s Guy Gardner are confirmed to reunite on the big screen in James Gunn’s upcoming sequel Man of Tomorrow. With the two Lanterns joining Superman and Lex Luthor to confront Brainiac, their presence in the 2027 movie pulls them from grounded terrestrial mysteries to world-scale war. New Lantern heroes and their respective villains could be introduced soon afterward, as their lore seems to be developing faster than that of any other DCU character.

Before Man of Tomorrow adds unbridled sci-fi and fantasy to John Stewart's DCU story, DC Comics shows how visually stunning a full-scale Green Lantern movie or show would look.

Hal Jordan’s clash against the returning Green Lantern villain Krona and the Manhunters in Green Lantern Corps showcases the perfect scale and tone missing from the DCU's Lanterns adaptation. In Green Lantern Corps #22, Hal Jordan teams up Krona, Jayna, and Tobi Y2K to dive deep into the wild Nexus of Emotions, where they meet face to face with the Manhunters. The heroes' varied costumes and powers, the Manhunters' colorful yet intimidating appearance, and the incredibly vibrant landscape of the Nexus of Emotions create a breathtaking visual experience that has never been adapted to live-action in a DC movie or show.

Although the HBO series Lanterns presents a bold and interesting take on the Greek Lantern mythos with its gritty crime-thriller murder mystery, the psychedelic visuals of Green Lantern Corps are something no live-action adaptation has dared to try. Now that Lanterns has introduced a new version of the Corps grounded on Earth, John Stewart's return in James Gunn's bombastic Man of Tomorrow and other future DCU Green Lantern appearances should draw inspiration from the comics and provide an unapologetically hard sci-fi story starring one or more Green Lanterns, especially if Nathan Fillion's scenery-chewing Guy Gardner has a key role in the plot.

Despite the immense popularity of the franchise, the Green Lantern Corps have yet to showcase their full cosmic majesty in live-action. On the big screen, the 2011 Green Lantern movie isolated Hal Jordan on a predictable hero's journey that barely scratched the surface of Oa's vast alien lore, Zack Snyder's Justice League only featured Yalan Gur in a single flashback scene, and James Gunn's Superman only included Guy Gardner in a supporting role. The DCU's Lanterns narrowed its scope and focused entirely on Hal Jordan and John Stewart's grounded mystery bound strictly to Earth.

DC is subtly revolutionizing the Green Lantern mythos, paving the way for the Corps to take a more prominent and completely fresh role.

A new Green Lantern big-screen adaptation could showcase multiple Earth Lanterns fighting side by side, complementing each other's constructs in creative team-up maneuvers with their own unique spin on their weapons. Audiences have yet to witness the sheer thrill of large-scale, live-action space warfare where hundreds of diverse ring-slingers engage in chaotic construct-filled battles against iconic Green Lantern villains such as the Sinestro Corps, the Black Lanterns, and the Manhunters. While the DCU presents a very different perspective, there's still room for a large-scale, colorful take on the Lanterns in the big or the small screen.

Which Green Lantern character would you want to see in live-action?

Green Lantern Corps #22 is available November 11 from DC Comics

10 Best YA Fantasy Books Of All Time

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Many of the most successful books of the last few decades have been YA fantasy novels. From supernatural romances to grand magical adventures, young readers have fallen so head-over-heels in love with the fictional worlds they never wanted to leave that the genre is one of the strongest in modern literature. What’s more, their appeal extends well beyond teenagers. The excitement of a solid literary fantasy adventure is entertaining no matter one's age, even if the commercially-targeted readership is explicitly young adults.

Countless novels will always be considered classics of the genre, but the best YA fantasy books stand apart, and their status is hard to argue against. Many have transcended their original readership to become the foundations of iconic pop culture properties. Whether they send readers to faerie courts, supernatural criminal underworlds, dystopian versions of earth, or hidden magic schools, the greatest YA fantasy novels account for some of the most exciting and emotionally engaging stories modern literature has to offer.

Few YA fantasy books understand the all-consuming intensity of first love as effectively as Twilight. Stephenie Meyer makes the supernatural romance between sparkling vampire Edward and new girl in town Bella feel more magical and awe-inspiring than any of the story’s supernatural elements. The intensity and directness of the couple’s romantic journey means readers are invested long before the deeper lore and fantasy worldbuilding fully take shape.

The four original Twilight novels also tap into common young-adult anxieties around belonging. The supernatural families are used to explore questions about loyalty and independence, giving Bella’s romantic choices consequences beyond simply choosing a partner. While there are certain elements of the later plot that became divisive, such as the idea of werewolf imprinting, the way Stephenie Meyer gives Bella’s uncertainty and longing as much of a presence as the series’ supernatural elements ensures it will always be a YA fantasy staple.

You can purchase Twilight, the first novel in the Twilight series by Stephenie Meyer, here.

Faerie courts are common territory in YA fantasy, but they rarely feel as hostile or full of danger like in Holly Black's The Folk of the Air novels. Beginning with 2018’s The Cruel Prince, the trilogy treats etiquette, bargains, and social standing as practical instruments of power just as important as any sword or spell. The central heroine of the series, Jude, is a mortal girl raised in this dangerous faerie world after her parents’ murder. Jude has ambitions beyond mere survival, and it’s here that The Folk of the Air novels gain their brilliance as reading experiences beyond their fantasy setting.

Unlike many YA fantasy heroes, Jude is openly aggressive and confrontational, and Holly Black’s willingness to embrace such an abrasive protagonist immediately distinguishes The Folk of the Air novels. Jude’s victory isn’t based on any kind of divine destiny. She’s not a humble ordinary person who finds themselves suddenly imbued with great power. Her success requires an open appetite for manipulation and seeing one’s foes defeated, a rarity among central YA fantasy characters. Anyone who enjoys worlds where a backhanded compliment is just as dangerous as a concealed dagger will find The Folk of the Air books among the most satisfying that YA fantasy has to offer.

You can purchase The Cruel Prince, the first novel in The Folk of the Air series by Holly Black, here.

Any decent heist story requires a memorable ensemble group of characters, especially when it blends with other genres. Leigh Bardugo completely understands this with her Six of Crows duology, which is part of the author’s Grishaverse universe. Bardugo uses the heist story structure to embark on an incredibly satisfying character-focused adventure, with the elaborate schemes of Kaz Brekker and his five talented specialists almost taking a secondary focus to the intense interpersonal drama between the crew’s members.

It’s incredibly impressive how emotional material weaves so seamlessly with criminal adventure, and this is why Six of Crows and its sequel, Crooked Kingdom, count among the best YA fantasy novels (and are definitely the best Grishaverse stories). Relationships and trust only develop through intense patience, testing and setting of boundaries, and difficult choices, which gives enormous weight to every new friendship or unexpected portrayal. Combined with the commercial brutality of the setting Ketterdam, this character-first approach leads to a YA fantasy crime story whose brilliance extends far beyond its twists.

You can purchase Six of Crows, the first novel in the Six of Crows duology by Leigh Bardugo, here.

The Harry Potter books make magical education feel like an experience readers have personally shared. Lessons, examinations, rivalries, meals, and school holidays make Hogwarts School of Witchcraft and Wizardry one of the most satisfying settings in any YA fantasy series. Across the seven Harry Potter novels, the Wizarding World becomes as fleshed out as any of the book’s characters. This leads to the central conflict over its fate between Harry and Voldemort feeling far more consequential and important than almost any other fictional battle between good and evil.

What’s more, while the worldbuilding of the Harry Potter books accounts for why they remain so beloved after decades, the story itself taps into many of the themes that define the best YA fantasy. The friendship between Harry, Ron, and Hermione remains the emotional foundation throughout, and this keeps the ever-expanding mythology grounded and ensures every victory and defeat resonates with readers. Few fantasy series balance the satisfaction of an individual school-year adventure with the momentum of a larger conflict quite so successfully.

You can purchase Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone, the first novel in the Harry Potter series by J.K. Rowling, here.

Lyrical prose can give literary fantasy tremendous atmosphere, and Daughter of Smoke & Bone brings this appeal to the YA corner of the genre. Author Laini Taylor’s imagery gives every setting, character, and creature a vivid physical presence, making the extraordinary feel real enough to touch. The opening novel introduces readers to Karou, a 17-year-old art student whose mysterious errands connect everyday Prague to a much stranger hidden world. The many twists, turns, and shock revelations give the story a lot of momentum, but it’s Taylor’s writing style that elevates the three Daughter Of Smoke & Bone novels from simply being great YA fantasy stories to among the greatest books in the genre.

There’s a lot of deep thematic work in the Daughter Of Smoke & Bone books too, especially when it comes to exploring which side is truly good and which is truly evil in a seemingly traditional fantasy conflict. Ideas like romance, grief, and vengeance also complicate matters and add a distinct spin to Karou’s journey and place in the wider battle between the angelic Seraphim and human-animal hybrid Chimaera. The result is a philosophically rich reading experience whose emotional depth is supported by some truly beautiful prose. Throw in a great magic system and a perfect tonal balance between its fantasy and romance and this secures itself as an essential reading for YA fantasy fan.

You can purchase Daughter of Smoke & Bone, the first novel in the Daughter of Smoke & Bone trilogy by Laini Taylor, here.

Rebellion forms the center of many YA fantasy narratives, but few examine how oppression is actually enforced quite like Sabaa Tahir’s An Ember in the Ashes quartet. Violence in Tahir’s Roman-inspired world is reinforced through education, punishment, status, and the constant narrowing of available choices, which the author explores with chilling detail. The plot places readers on both sides of the oppressor-oppressed divide, with the story split between scholar Laia’s attempts to rescue her imprisoned brother from the regime, and soldier Elias’s questioning of the social structure he helps to violently enforce.

The alternating viewpoints of Laia and Elias become an incredibly effective lens through which the An Ember in the Ashes novels explore the relationship between freedom and rebellion. The world of the Martial Empire is a brutal one, but the brutality has a thematic purpose. However, that doesn’t mean that the books are an unending parade of bleakness. Tahir also understands the importance of tenderness within such a harsh setting. The darker passages are all complemented by moments of affection and compassion that serve to make Laia and Elias’s resistance to the status quo matter all the more, especially as the scale and supernatural aspects of the plot increase.

You can purchase An Ember in the Ashes, the first novel in the An Ember in the Ashes quartet by Sabaa Tahir, here.

Humor is one of YA fantasy’s most underrated aspects, and no book series in the genre use it as effectively as Percy Jackson & the Olympians. Author Rick Riordan makes ancient gods hilariously fit the present-day world by translating the vanity, grudges, and family disputes that defined them in Ancient Greek myths into modern behavior. However, comedy isn’t the only reason Percy Jackson’s saga is now one of YA fiction's most iconic fantasy adventures. The series earns its place through the way it makes the emotional conflicts and problems faced by its young heroes while on their grand quest resonate perfectly with the target readership.

As Percy and his grow up across the 7 Percy Jackson novels, they must confront harsh truths like loyalty becoming more difficult when enemies have legitimate grievances, and that the journey to becoming a hero never comes without compromise or sacrifice. Its treatment of ADHD and dyslexia is especially memorable, reframing experiences associated with classroom frustration within an empowering fantasy framework. Rick Riordan never lets these ideas overwhelm the entertainment, either. The narrative voice remains conversational and quick, making exposition unusually painless and even enjoyable. It’s a perfect blend of lighthearted jokes, genuinely engaging fantasy adventure, and a core moral message that struggling to fit in never means one doesn’t matter.

You can purchase The Lightning Thief, the first novel in the Percy Jackson & the Olympians series by Rick Riordan, here.

The best urban fantasy novels depend on making familiar places feel newly charged with possibility, and The Mortal Instruments delivers that pleasure like few other YA book series. Nightclubs, streets, and buildings become entrances to a supernatural society with its own laws, prejudices, and competing loyalties. Beginning with City of Bones, Cassandra Clare’s six-book series follows teenager Clary as she discovers that she is a Shadowhunter, a human-angel hybrid. Clare builds outward from this premise to create a unique fantasy world that becomes the perfect setting to use fantasy conflict as a tool to examine emotional disorder and trauma.

The Mortal Instruments novels cover almost all the staple thematic topics associated with YA fantasy like friendships, romantic attachments, and complicated family histories. It’s how Cassandra Clare meshes them into a rich hidden fantasy world that incorporates dozens of supernatural species and cultures that makes the series a genre essential. While its unapologetically melodramatic tone does put some readers off, the way this intensity is used to explore ideas belonging and inherited prejudice is incredibly engaging. Even at their most extravagant, The Mortal Instruments remain among the most emotionally poignant YA fantasy series ever written, and they remain an absolute highlight of the wider genre.

You can purchase City of Bones, the first novel in The Mortal Instruments series by Cassandra Clare, here.

Philip Pullman’s His Dark Materials trilogy isn’t just a YA fantasy achievement, it’s a literary triumph. Not only is its fantasy worldbuilding unique and substantial and its approach to coming-of-age YA themes pitch-perfect, it’s also one of the most philosophically rich book trilogies of the modern era. The core of all three novels is the conflict between the free will of the individual and the assumed absolute authority of fate and destiny, with nearly every aspect and detail serving Pullman’s exploration. For example, the dæmons of protagonist Lyra’s world are much more than animal companions all humans are born with. They’re literal manifestations of the human soul, with their animal forms representing a person’s personality.

The fixed forms of these dæmons then naturally lead to questions surrounding whether someone’s characteristics are immutable, or whether they can eventually change. For a YA novel, this is extraordinary mature emotional territory, especially as the narrative unfolds and it becomes clear that Lyra and Will, the companion she finds in another world, may end up having to kill a figure who is god in all but name. Philip Pullman does a remarkable job of trusting young readers to engage with ideas like religious authority and ethical responsibility without ever talking down to them. At the same time, fantastical elements like armored bears, witches, and knives that can tear holes in reality ensure that the philosophical argument takes place within the confines of a riveting adventure.

You can purchase The Golden Compass, the first novel in the His Dark Materials trilogy by Philip Pullman, here.

While the world of The Hunger Games draws from dystopian sci-fi with its aesthetic, its story structure and the grand quest of the bow-and-arrow wielding Katniss Everdeen not only make it a perfect YA fantasy series, but one of the best in the entire genre. Author Suzanne Collins takes an immediately gripping survival premise and uses it to explore the role of entertainment in enforcing inequality and unjust political power. It’s a deeply relevant thematic focus for modern readers, especially compared with more traditional and morally clear-cut good vs. evil conflicts. It’s for this reason that The Hunger Games novels are, for many, the definitive example of YA fantasy at its most engaging.

However, there’s a lot more to The Hunger Games than simply tapping into the concerns of contemporary readers. The story is gripping throughout, and the way that Suzanne Collins refuses to treat the central rebellion as an inherent force-for-good that’s beyond scrutiny adds a moral complexity that ensures readers feel respected. Collins writes with a directness that makes these ideas accessible without making them simplistic. Combine this with the fact that the Games themselves make for some incredibly high-octane action sequences and Katniss being an incredibly well-written heroine, and The Hunger Games novels make their claim to being one of the best YA fantasy book series incredibly difficult to dispute.

You can purchase The Hunger Games, the first novel in The Hunger Games series by Suzanne Collins, here.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Why The Boeing 777-9 Is Not A Straight Replacement For Every Airbus A380

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

minhanphotos & Tom Buysse | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

Ethan is an aviation professional with a degree in Aviation Management with Flight from the Florida Institute of Technology. He previously wrote aviation and tourism features for Florida Tech’s student newspaper, The Crimson, where he developed a clear, reader‑focused reporting style. Aviation journalism has remained a consistent priority throughout his career.

He currently holds a Restricted ATP and Commercial Multi‑Engine Pilot certificate and works in aviation supply chain and aircraft component repair. Alongside his industry role, he is pursuing his Certified Flight Instructor rating and actively building flight experience.

A lifelong aviation enthusiast, Ethan spent countless hours at Raleigh–Durham International Airport’s observation deck studying air traffic and practicing amateur photography and videography.

Having been manufactured from 2003 until 2021, the Airbus A380 currently does not have a direct replacement available on the open market. The program itself was designed as a competitive option to a similar, superjumbo aircraft, the Boeing 747. The A380 has the distinction of being the highest capacity commercial jet ever produced, with its most dense example operated by Emirates, at 615 seats.

Due to a shift in a broader market trend of lower capacity, with less dense aircraft being prioritized by airlines, the A380 and 747 are left without a direct replacement. The closest possible successors to this type of jet include both the Airbus A350 and Boeing 777 families, both with high-capacity variants that approach, but do not exceed 500 seats. Some companies that opted for a purchase of the A380 during its production run made the unit a significant part of the network and fleet strategy; sourcing a direct replacement to fill the exact same niche has proven to be difficult for many.

On seat count alone, typical A380 layouts contain 500–600 seats, a window that no aircraft currently in production can comfortably touch with a multi-class layout. Airbus' official product page outlines that the A380 has a whopping maximum certified capacity of 853 seats, a figure that has not been touched by any airline in its operating history. Emirates' previous 615-seat configuration included the signature onboard experience that its customers are used to, at 32 inches (81.28 cm) of legroom. Mathematically speaking, an 843-seat A380 solicits significantly less legroom onboard. Comfort is not the goal with such a layout, but rather maximum capacity at the minimum legal threshold.

Typical 777-9 layouts are around 400–430 seats, with more than one cabin onboard. Flight Global reported back in 2018 that Korean Air once configured an A380 at 407 seats, in an all-business class layout, marking the lowest density example known to have flown. However, this example was exceptional, as it was primarily geared toward select missions as a charter.

Real-world seating differences by airline are of note. Regardless of an airline's configuration, whether it is a premium heavy model like France's La Compagnie, or an all economy 777 layout aboard IndiGo, the A380 simply has more square footage and a higher rated maximum capacity than the 777-9. While an airline can utilize a consistent ratio of economy seats to premium seats onboard, the total number of seats once available on an A380 route cannot be evenly transferred to the 777-9.

Why would an airline ever willingly choose fewer seats? Several different factors are involved, relating back to the four forces of flight: weight, lift, thrust, and drag. A smaller aircraft generally weighs less, requiring less lift to stay airborne and less thrust to maintain cruise speed. Generating that thrust requires burning fuel, while producing additional lift inevitably creates drag that must also be overcome.

Engine count alone does not explain the fuel-burn gap. According to Aero Corner, the 777-9's two General Electric GE9X engines each generate roughly 110,000 pounds of thrust, making them among the most powerful commercial jet engines ever developed. By comparison, the A380's Rolls-Royce Trent 900 and Engine Alliance GP7200 engines are typically rated between 70,000 and 84,000 pounds of thrust each. Combined with advances in bypass ratio, materials, and wing design, the 777-9 can achieve similar mission capabilities while relying on only two engines rather than four.

Boeing claims significant improvements through GE9X and aerodynamics; about 10% lower fuel burn than the Boeing 777-300ER

The A380's maximum takeoff weight (MTOW) is 1.27 million lb (575,000 kg), whereas the 777-9's MTOW is 775,000 lb (351,500 kg). The A380's MTOW is nearly double that of the Boeing, thus requiring nearly double the thrust to lift off and maintain flight. Further, the 777-9 offers a very high aspect-ratio composite wing, folding wingtips, improved lift-to-drag ratio, and fewer engine nacelles to provide an aerodynamic edge over the A380. With a larger wing span, at 235 feet, 5 inches (71.80 meters), it also benefits from an improved lift-to-drag ratio and lower induced drag.

The Airbus A380 can fly up to 16 hours nonstop. Here’s how its massive fuel capacity and range make ultra-long-haul flights possible.

If the objective is moving the maximum possible number of passengers through a slot-constrained airport such as London Heathrow Airport (LHR) or Tokyo Narita Airport (NRT), the A380 remains unmatched. On a high-demand route backed up by the high capacity of a full A380, the choice becomes economical for the operator.

However, airlines besides the A380 giants of Emirates, Qatar Airways, or British Airways do not optimize their fleets around a handful of peak-demand routes. Instead, such companies seek aircraft that can generate profits across a dynamic variety of markets, seasons, and demand conditions. This is where the 777-9's lower fuel consumption, reduced maintenance burden, and greater operational flexibility begin to outweigh its lower seating capacity.

An aircraft that burns approximately 2,000 fewer gallons (7570.82 liters) of fuel per hour than an A380 can often produce a stronger financial result even while transporting fewer passengers. This conclusion is only augmented during times of economic uncertainty and high fuel costs. Now more than ever, due to the Iran conflict, fuel efficiency is undeniably important. With an aviation marketplace that increasingly renders the A380 more and more difficult to operate profitably, where does its niche lie today?

In practice, airlines often use the 777-9 not as a replacement for an individual A380 flight but as a replacement for the role the A380 once played within a network. That distinction is important. Network planners evaluate total market demand, connecting traffic flows, slot availability, and seasonal variation rather than focusing exclusively on aircraft size.

Consider Emirates, Lufthansa, or Qatar Airways. Their largest hubs feed passengers from dozens or even hundreds of routes into long-haul banks. During peak periods, an A380's enormous capacity can be valuable. A380s are typically deployed on routes from Dubai International Airport (DXB), Frankfurt Airport (FRA), and Doha International Airport (DOH) respectively, to global hubs worldwide, like London and John F. Kennedy International Airport (JFK). The 777 family can still reliably operate such routes, just not at the same scale as the A380.

A 777-9 gives airlines greater flexibility. During off seasons or weaker demand cycles, those same seats on the A380 become harder to sell profitably. Routes that may support 550 passengers in July might only support 400 in February. Rather than maintaining year-round A380 service, carriers can better match capacity to demand while preserving network connectivity.

What do you think is the real reason Airbus discontinued the A380?

The results of the 777 among other twin engine widebodies are visible throughout the industry. While Airbus ended A380 production in 2021 after delivering 251 aircraft, the Boeing 777X program accumulated hundreds of orders before entering commercial service. Emirates alone ordered more than 200 aircraft from the 777X family, despite remaining the world's largest A380 operator, serving as a strong signal that A380 operators are looking at the 777X program as a makeshift replacement.

Even airlines that continue to fly the superjumbo, including Lufthansa and British Airways, increasingly rely on twin-engine widebodies such as the 777, Boeing 787, and Airbus A350 families for the majority of their long-haul operations. Rather than replacing the A380 route-for-route, modern twinjets have largely replaced the network strategy the aircraft was designed around.

The success of aircraft such as the 777-300ER and later the 787 demonstrated that airlines often preferred operating more frequent flights with smaller aircraft rather than concentrating hundreds of passengers onto a single departure. The 777-9 represents the latest evolution of that philosophy, combining near-A380 range and premium-cabin capability with significantly lower fuel consumption and maintenance requirements.

By default, the end of the A380's production has forced customers to look elsewhere for an ideal replacement. With no commercial quad-jets currently available for purchase from either Airbus or Boeing, the market has pivoted to the highest capacity airframes in production. Fuel efficiency has become not only an added benefit of modern airliners, but also a reason for airlines today to continue the operation and, in some cases, as with Ethiopian Airlines, upgrade its orders.

The market response to each aircraft highlights the industry's shift toward efficient twin-engine widebodies. Airbus reports that it ultimately secured 251 firm orders for the A380 before ending production, while Boeing's 777-9 had accumulated 546 firm orders as of September despite not yet entering commercial service. Using each manufacturer's published list prices, those order totals equate to approximately $111.9 billion in potential A380 sales revenue and roughly $241.4 billion for the 777-9 alone.

While airlines rarely pay published list prices, the disparity nevertheless illustrates how strongly carriers have favored Boeing's large twin-engine strategy over Airbus' superjumbo approach. Boeing has already beaten the A380 in total production revenue, prior to the 777-9's first delivery. This is a solid indication of the program's success that is yet to come, and the broader market trend shifting to highly efficient twinjets in lieu of superjumbos.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

The best of Simple Flying, directly in your inbox.

Commander Shepard’s Actor Accidentally Immortalized One Mass Effect Race as Steve Buscemi

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Mark Meer thought he'd just make alien noises for Mass Effect before he was cast as Shepard

Mark Meer never planned on saving the universe, but the universe had other plans.

You know Meer from a lengthy association with BioWare’s most popular RPGs. He’s lent his voice to several of the studio’s games, from Neverwinter Nights to Jade Empire. Most famously, he voiced the male version of Mass Effect’s Commander Shepard, but his work with BioWare dates back to one of the studio’s earliest breakout hits.

“I did one line in Baldur's Gate 2, and then subsequently, BioWare just kept hiring me for every subsequent project leading up to the Mass Effect trilogy,” Meer said.

In the wake of EA shutting down BioWare, laying off its remaining staff, and canceling Mass Effect: Universe, Polygon spoke with Jennifer Hale and Meer, the voices of Mass Effect’s Commander Shepard. They talked about their hopes for the future of Mass Effect, but also looked backward. Both actors recorded for the studio’s Baldur’s Gate games. Meer would play “monsters and antagonists and that sort of thing.”

They also both had roles in BioWare’s Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic. Hale notably played Bastila Shan, while Meer voiced Carth Onasi, but only in KOTOR’s demo. The role in the full game eventually went to Raphael Sbarge, who, as it happens, ended up playing Kaidan Alenko in Mass Effect. (“Because he replaced me, that's why I killed Kaiden on Virmire,” Meer likes to joke.)

While Mass Effect was still in its concept art stage, he was brought in to aid in a presentation for the developers about how the aliens they were creating might sound. “I don't know if people realize how instrumental Mark was in defining the sound of every single species in this game,” Hale said, shouting out her friend and fellow Shepard.

“So what does a typical salarian sound like? What does a typical krogan sound like? That kind of thing,” Meer said. “It was nice to see some of the advice that I gave or some of the suggestions I made get implemented.

“I do take credit for the salarians all having the cadence of Steve Buscemi,” Meer added with a laugh. The first salarian he did was essentially a Buscemi impression for a demo, and it stuck.

While recording all those aliens, BioWare asked Meer if he’d audition for Shepard. “I thought there was absolutely no way I would get it,” Meer said, “which is probably good because I was not nervous at all.” He thought it would be “impossible” for him to land the role, and after auditioning he could “go back to doing my alien stuff.”

Of course, that wasn’t the case. He still did record “a bunch of aliens” for Mass Effect, like the vorcha, but that work was secondary to his role as Shepard. He was thrilled, but also somewhat shocked — and that shock doubled when he learned he’d be doing the same role as Hale, a titan of the industry. “It was sort of like being told, ‘You've been cast in this movie in the lead, and Meryl Streep will be playing opposite you playing the same character.’”

“Getting to be the lead in one of these was really a dream come true.”

Hale noted that Meer “set the tone” for Shepard by recording the initial couple sessions first. He was in Edmonton with BioWare while Hale recorded her lines in Los Angeles. In the early going, Hale’s LA team would play for her whatever Meer had recorded before she’d tackle her lines. His performance would influence the direction Hale took. She’d record a line multiple ways, including imitating Meer’s performance and then giving her own spin on a line read.

Eventually they got into a groove and, because of the way schedules work out, Meer wouldn’t always record first. Regardless of who was up, however, BioWare did have a specific request: “The one thing that they did really stress was you have to take the exact same amount of time to say the line,” Meer said with a laugh.

While Hale isn’t a gamer herself (“I suck at it,” she joked), Meer is a big fan of choice-based RPGs. “Getting to be the lead in one of these was really a dream come true,” he said.

The player is given a lot of flexibility in customizing Shepard, and Mass Effect’s Paragon and Renegade morality system allows them even more freedom in creating the hero. “So we really had to find the core of Shepard. What is the same between Renegade and Paragon?” Meer said.

He compared a Paragon Shepard to an upstanding Paladin in Dungeons & Dragons, and a Renegade Shepard to a “sociopath [who’s] still saving the galaxy, but not being polite or empathetic about it in any way whatsoever.” No matter what, Meer, Hale, and their directors settled on Shepard being a “military officer that's used to giving commands under pressure and is very professional in that sense,” Meer said.

Both Paragon and Renegade are worth playing, and both have their moments to shine. However, the freedom of a Renegade Shepard strikes a certain cord with both actors. “I always joke that Paragon's who I wish I was and Renegade's what I wish I could say,” Hale said. “I think we all aspire to be Paragon, but sometimes wish we could be Renegade,” Meer added.

Because of the Paragon and Renegade system — and because these games are just so damn good — the community loves jumping back into Mass Effect for a new playthrough, regardless of if they make new choices or roll with the same ones as always.

“You know, next year will be the 20th anniversary of the first Mass Effect game,” Meer said. “The fact that we are still talking about it, that we still have fans that are so passionate about that universe — it is impressive and humbling.”

'This game gave so many people the feeling of being represented'

MGM’s 113-Minute Sci-Fi Thriller Officially Lands on Streaming

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Ridley Scott's Alien introduced audiences to one of the most recognizable monsters in movie history, inspiring decades of filmmakers to come up with their own horrifying extraterrestrial creations. That led to an army of copycat movies, all of which tried to varying levels of success to replicate the thrills, the spills, the scares and the... sex? Wait, what? Back in the 1990s, MGM decided to take the idea of an alien invasion in a rather different direction, combining science fiction, body horror, and an extremely dangerous leading lady into a movie that became a surprisingly enormous box office hit.

The 1995 sci-fi horror thriller Species has officially arrived on Prime Video in the United States, joining the streaming service on November 1, 31 years after its theatrical debut. Directed by Roger Donaldson (The Recruit), the movie earned more than $113 million worldwide against a reported $35 million production budget, eventually spawning several sequels, as well as inspiring an army of teenage boys to rent the movie over and over again from their local Blockbuster in the mid 1990s.

When a beautiful human-alien hybrid escapes from observation, scientist Xavier Fitch dispatches a crew of experts to find her before she is able to fulfill her horrific purpose: to mate with unsuspecting men and produce offspring that could destroy mankind. As her deadly biological clock ticks rapidly, Fitch and his team are hurled into a desperate battle in which the fate of humanity itself hangs in the balance. The movie is one of those kitschy, guilty pleasures from the 1990s that, while also science fiction, would also probably classify as soft-core. And that's why it did so well, presumably!

The cast includes Natasha Henstridge (Ghosts of Mars) as Sil, Ben Kingsley (Gandhi) as Xavier Fitch, Michael Madsen (Reservoir Dogs) as Preston Lennox, Forest Whitaker (The Last King of Scotland) as Dan Smithson, Marg Helgenberger (CSI: Crime Scene Investigation) as Laura Baker, Alfred Molina (Spider-Man 2) as Dr. Stephen Arden, Michelle Williams (The Fabelmans) as young Sil, and Whip Hubley (Top Gun) as John Carey. Species earned approximately $113.4 million worldwide, including $60.1 million domestically. The movie holds a 42% critics' score and 31% audience score on Rotten Tomatoes.

Species is streaming now on Prime Video in the United States. Stay tuned to Collider for all the latest news and updates on the biggest and best movies and shows heading to your favorite streaming platforms soon.

US Adds 165,000 Jobs in October: Why Bitcoin Traders Are Watching the Fed

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: The US economy added 165,000 payroll jobs in October while unemployment stood at 4.1%, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics’ November 6 release. The data complicate hopes of quick monetary easing, but the report by itself cannot establish what the Federal Reserve will do next or where Bitcoin will trade.

Bitcoin’s difficult trading week has coincided with a closely watched update on the US economy. The Bureau of Labor Statistics reported on November 6 that nonfarm payroll employment increased by 165,000 in October. The unemployment rate was little changed at 4.1%.

For cryptocurrency traders, the significance is not that payrolls have a mechanical relationship with Bitcoin’s price. Rather, employment data are one part of the evidence the Federal Reserve weighs when deciding whether inflation and economic activity justify changing interest rates.

The BLS said employment increased in professional and business services, healthcare, and transportation and warehousing. Professional and business services added 70,000 jobs, substantially above that category’s recent monthly average. The report also revised August and September gains upward by a combined 84,000 jobs.

Those revisions matter because monthly payroll estimates are not fixed snapshots of economic reality. More complete information can materially alter the picture after the first publication. Anyone building a large market argument on a single month should therefore examine the trend and the revisions, not only the latest number.

Average hourly earnings for private-sector employees rose by 0.4% in October and 3.1% over 12 months, according to the same release. Wage developments can affect the inflation outlook, although inflation depends on many other factors including productivity, goods costs and service-sector conditions.

When investors expect a lower path for policy rates, the relative attraction of cash and short-duration interest-bearing assets may fall, potentially supporting risk assets. A stronger labour market can complicate that narrative by giving policymakers less reason to ease quickly. But the Fed’s response depends on the whole data set, not an automatic rule that more jobs always mean higher rates.

Bitcoin is not a conventional corporate security with earnings that can be discounted using a single valuation model. Even so, it trades within financial markets where leverage costs, dollar liquidity and investor risk appetite change with the broader macroeconomic environment. That connection can become particularly visible during sharp stock-market moves.

There is also a separate institutional channel. NewsBTC reported that spot Bitcoin ETFs suffered $398.6 million in net withdrawals on November 2, reflecting flows into and out of listed investment products. Those fund numbers are useful but do not show why each investor traded.

Market commentary has linked recent cryptocurrency weakness to technology-stock volatility and changing interest-rate expectations. That can be a plausible interpretation of contemporaneous movements, but correlation is not proof of one exclusive cause. Leverage, derivatives positioning and crypto-specific events may be operating at the same time.

The important next readings include inflation releases, future employment revisions and Federal Reserve communications. Traders should also follow the distinction between network fundamentals and investment-market behaviour explored in NewsBTC’s hashrate coverage. A shift in financial conditions can affect market prices long before it changes the underlying protocol.

For now, October’s jobs report provides a firmer data point in the interest-rate debate. It does not supply a confident Bitcoin price target or a guaranteed forecast for the next Fed decision.

This article was written by the News Desk and edited by Samuel Rae.

Walter Koenig Wanted A Star Trek: The Next Generation Role That Wasn't Pavel Chekov

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

While "Star Trek: The Next Generation" is set 100 years after "Star Trek: The Original Series," some "TOS" cast members appeared on the show. In the pilot, "Encounter at Farpoint," a 137-year-old Dr. McCoy (DeForest Kelley) visits the Enterprise-D and gives Data (Brent Spiner) some sound advice: "You treat her like a lady, she'll always bring you home."

Later, in the Season 3 episode "Sarek," Mark Lenard guest-starred as the eponymous Vulcan ambassador, also known for being Spock's (Leonard Nimoy) father. Spock himself then appeared in the Season 5 two-parter "Reunification," where it's revealed he's moved to Romulus to try and bring the Vulcans and Romulans together again as one. After that, the former Enterprise Chief Engineer Scotty (James Doohan) appeared in the "Next Generation" Season 6 Episode "Relics," having spent several decades trapped in a Dyson sphere. Finally, Captain Kirk (William Shatner) teamed up with Captain Picard (Patrick Stewart) in the 1994 spin-off film "Star Trek Generations."

That lineup leaves out George Takei (Sulu), Nichelle Nichols (Uhura), and Walter Koenig (Chekov), who died this past October right before Stewart did. The closest Koenig came to appearing on "The Next Generation" was in "Star Trek: Generations." In a scene set in 2294, Captain Kirk, Scotty, and Chekov attend the maiden voyage of the Enterprise-B. But Koenig had a pitch to appear on "Next Generation," which he shared in the October 1989 issue of "Starlog" magazine:

"I don't want to do a walk-on. I have an idea for an opening moment. Somebody has just come aboard the ship. They're talking about him in general. He turns around and it's [my] face. They say, 'Well, Commander Chekov.' 'No,' I say, 'Lt. Commander Chekov, Pavel Chekov's son.' It's a great opening."

"Star Trek" is no stranger at all to reusing the same actors to play different roles, including across series. Majel Barrett (wife of "Star Trek" creator Gene Roddenberry), who played Nurse Chapel on the original series, also had a recurring part on "Next Generation" as Ambassador Lwaxana Troi, mother of Counselor Deanna Troi (Marina Sirtis).

It's worth noting that Walter Koenig specifically wanted a part worth playing — "Not a walk-on" as he put it. Joining "Star Trek" in Season 2, Chekov was invariably the most overlooked member of the cast. One of his biggest parts in the "Trek" movies was in "Wrath of Khan," and that whole role is built on a continuity error: Khan Noonien Singh (Ricardo Montalbán) and Chekov know each other even though Koenig wasn't part of the "Star Trek" cast during Khan's first appearance, "Space Seed." Koenig later claimed he caught the error but, tellingly, stayed silent to protect his screentime.

Koenig often said he preferred his role on "Babylon 5," a different sci-fi series where he played the sinister psychic Alfred Bester, over playing Chekov because Bester was a much more substantive role. Note that "Starlog" interview was in 1989, years before "Babylon 5," so it's no surprise he was hungry for meaty roles.

"Star Trek: Picard" Season 3 eventually used Koenig's idea to give him a voiceover cameo as Anton Chekov, son of Pavel and the Federation's president. ("Anton" doubles as a homage to the late Anton Yelchin, who played Chekov in the 2009 "Star Trek" film and its sequels.) Sadly, though, that sort of Easter Egg cameo is exactly the kind of appearance Koenig had said he didn't want to settle for back in 1989.
