Your old iPod could be a sleeper flash drive

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

If you have an old iPod lying around, here’s how you can turn it into a cool flash drive.

Apple discontinued the iPod lineup years ago, but there are still many people who have one or more lying around — whether because they appreciate the nostalgia of using an MP3 player in the age of streaming or simply forgot them in a drawer. But even if you don't want to use your iPods to listen to music, they can still have a second life as a cute (and somewhat hidden) way to store files and transfer them between computers, as pre-iOS iPods can function as a basic USB flash drive that works on both Mac and Windows.

Just don't rush out to buy an old iPod if you don't already have one, though. A 32GB USB flash drive usually costs around $10 and offers much more storage than most older iPods, which have less than 20GB of storage — iPod Classic aside. Plus, they're all limited to USB 2 speeds, so they aren't great for transferring large files. But if you have an iPod lying around and don't know what to do with it, this is a good way to reuse it.

To use your iPod as a flash drive, you first need to enable Disk Use, which is off by default. On a Mac, connect your iPod and look for it in the Finder sidebar. Go to the General tab and then click Enable Disk Use in the options. Before closing the Finder window, click Apply. On Windows, you can use either iTunes or the Apple Devices app to enable this feature — the process is the same.

Once you enable Disk Use, your iPod will appear in the Finder or File Explorer just like any other USB flash drive or external hard drive. Drag any files you want onto it, and that's it — they'll be stored on your iPod. These files won't be visible on the iPod itself, only on a computer or other devices that can read external drives. Your files will share space with the iPod's system files and the music already on it, so you won't have the full storage capacity available to use as a flash drive.

An iPod set up on a Mac is usually formatted as HFS+, and Windows can't read that file system without third-party software. For wider compatibility, restore the iPod and set it up using a Windows PC so it uses the FAT32 file system. Macs can read FAT32 just fine, as can smart TVs and other platforms, although it is limited to 4GB per file. Do bear in mind that restoring your iPod will erase everything on it.

Older iPods also have a feature called Disk Mode, which is not the same as Disk Use. Disk Mode is essentially a diagnostic tool and can help when an iPod freezes or doesn't show up on your computer. On click wheel models, including the iPod classic, iPod mini, iPod with video and nano up to the fifth generation, flip the Hold switch on and off. Then press and hold Menu and the center button for about six seconds until the Apple logo appears. Let go, then immediately hold the center button and Play/Pause until the Disk Mode screen appears. Laying the iPod flat and pressing Play/Pause near the outer edge of the wheel makes it easier to reach.

Neither Disk Mode nor Disk Use is available on the iPod touch, since it runs iOS instead of the iPod's older operating system. These features are only available on iPods with a Click Wheel and the 7th generation iPod nano (the one with a touch screen). Also, if you plan to use your iPod as a portable flash drive, be sure to bring a 30-pin cable with you, since you're not likely to find one in the wild these days. If reusing your old iPod as a flash drive isn't quite enough, check out the Sprout Pi Mainboard, a crowdfunded project that adds modern features like USB-C, Bluetooth, and even streaming apps to select iPod models.

Ex-FBI agent pleads guilty to stealing $2.47 million in crypto from wallets tied to 35 investigations

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Court filings show Patrick Yaroch drained at least 79 wallets, including some belonging to crime victims, and planned to use the money to buy a vineyard in Portugal.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch admitted on Thursday to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency from wallets tied to FBI investigations, according to the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Eastern District of Virginia.

Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to one count each of wire fraud and money laundering related to the thefts. Each count carries up to 20 years in prison, and sentencing is set for March 4, 2027.

Beginning no later than December 2024, Yaroch searched FBI databases, including a classified one, for crypto credentials, according to the statement of facts he signed. He used the information he found to make about 100 transfers out of at least 79 wallets connected to 35 FBI investigations.

Most of the wallets belonged to people under FBI investigation, and some were controlled by two adversarial nation states the filing does not name. NBC News reported in August, citing two sources, that the adversarial nation in the original complaint was Russia. Yaroch also made at least 10 transfers from wallets belonging to crime victims in other FBI cases, and at least one from a wallet the FBI had funded for an operation.

The filing calls Yaroch "indiscriminate," with individual thefts ranging from 81 cents to more than $580,000. After each theft, he kept checking FBI systems to make sure no one had noticed.

Yaroch joined the FBI's Boston Division in 2017, working on a squad focused on one of the two nation states. From February 2025 until July 31, 2026, he was a supervisory special agent in the Counterintelligence and Espionage Division at FBI headquarters.

Yaroch had racked up significant debts before the scheme began: he had taken out more than $100,000 in loans in 2024 and owed more than $45,000 on credit cards, according to the filing.

In one example from the filing, Yaroch used credentials from a criminal case file on Nov. 6, 2025, to empty two wallets into his own bitcoin wallet. Over the following weeks, he ran the BTC through a swap service with privacy features, converted some of it to Monero (XMR) and then Solana (SOL), and moved the funds through a Sui wallet to his Kraken account.

Between Nov. 25, 2025, and Jan. 13, 2026, Yaroch moved about $93,082 to a TD Bank account and used it to pay down debts, including about $30,176 on credit cards and $20,000 toward a Lexus, per the filing. Yaroch initially said he stole the crypto out of frustration, but prosecutors wrote in an August court filing that he "should not be viewed as a selfless Robin Hoodesque figure."

On June 4, Yaroch asked ChatGPT what it would do with about $1 million if it wanted to leave the U.S. and live in a European Union country. The chatbot's answer cited his wife and child, "the desire for a 2-5 hectare estate, interest in age-worthy red wine," and named Portugal "my top choice for your specific situation," according to the filing.

On June 15, Yaroch gave two Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to register him with the country's tax authority. He also arranged a September trip to Portugal tied to his planned vineyard purchase. Prosecutors also said in the August filing that Yaroch had failed to report a 10-day trip in May, when he flew to Germany and came home from Portugal.

The prosecutors asked to keep him jailed before trial, citing about $160,000 he still held on Kraken and possible Espionage Act charges that were never filed. U.S. District Judge Anthony Trenga instead released him on a $50,000 bond with location monitoring, under conditions that bar him from using video game consoles.

Yaroch initially agreed to give agents a piece of paper that appeared to contain his seed phrase, according to the August filing. However, he later asked for it back after agents told him he had the right to stay silent and that what he said could be used against him in a criminal case.

When agents found the wallet he had pointed them to, $1.02 million had already moved out. Yaroch then admitted the funds were on the lending platform Suilend and helped them reinstall a wallet app he had deleted from his phone.

Under the plea agreement, Yaroch must pay at least $2.4 million in restitution and faces a forfeiture judgment of at least $2.4 million. He also agreed to forfeit 17,990.67 USDC and about 1.38 million SUI seized on July 31, worth roughly $1.2 million at current prices, plus the contents of his TD Bank, Charles Schwab, Kraken and BloFin accounts.

Yaroch acknowledged that the full proceeds of the scheme "are not available." He also agreed to be interviewed by Justice Department and FBI officials about his crimes.

If there's eventually a Netflix documentary about the case, Yaroch won't be profiting: any money he makes from books, films, articles or interviews about his FBI work or his case will go to the U.S. government. He must also submit any such work to the FBI for review before publication.

The Block reported in August that Yaroch had been fired on July 31 and charged with transporting and receiving stolen money. At the time, the Justice Department's Office of the Inspector General said he had moved about $1 million in roughly 10 transfers and had reported himself to two Justice Department employees on July 29.

Earlier this year, the FBI arrested John Daghita in Saint Martin over the alleged theft of more than $46 million in crypto from the U.S. Marshals Service. Onchain investigator ZachXBT alleged that Daghita is the son of an executive at a firm contracted to help the agency manage seized crypto.

A federal grand jury in Alexandria indicted Daghita on 15 counts in March, and a French appeals court in Guadeloupe approved his extradition to the U.S. in May, according to local broadcaster RCI. His arraignment is set for Nov. 12 in Alexandria, according to his court docket.

Lando Norris Extends São Paulo Pole Streak Ahead of Brazilian Grand Prix

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Norris will start the Brazilian Grand Prix from pole position for the third straight year as George Russell furiously misses out on pole before grid penalty.

Lando Norris secured his 20th career pole the weekend after he officially passed on the championship title to Kimi Antonelli.

Antonelli will join Norris on the front row, qualifying third and moving up a spot after his teammate George Russell received a grid penalty.

Russell, who was fastest in Q2 and fastest through sectors 1 and 2 in the final qualifying session as well, was told to back off on his final lap as the rain, which plagued the entire qualifying, picked up again. Russell was running up on Alex Albon’s Williams also in a flying lap and radioed for his former teammate to move out of the way. He was then called back potential in hopes of staying out for another out lap and hitting his second flyer on a wet but clearer track.

Furious over the radio to his race engineer, Marcus Dudley, Russell raged about being called off. Russell’s provisional pole of 1:27.674 was beaten by Norris by 0.204 seconds on the 2025 Drivers' Champion's second lap, and the English Mercedes driver was convinced that his next lap would be a step better.

“Mate! Russell yelled. “You told me to back off. What the hell?”

“George, you’re P2,” Toto Wolff replied over the radio.

With six minutes to go in the final stage, Russell was called to the garage and told to stay in the car just in case they could get out for a final out lap and flying lap. Dejected, his qualifying ended here, and his starting position moved from second to seventh with his five-place grid penalty.

I’m P2, but I don’t want to be P2; I want to be P1,” Russell radioed again.

While the difference between a sixth and seventh place start at Sao Paulo is small, Russell felt cheated out of a pole, which would still be recorded even with his grid penalty.

“Yeah, I mean, obviously congrats to Lando, but yeah, that was definitely the pole lap,” Russell said. “Misjudgment on our side; we need to understand what happened. Obviously, the rain was coming, and we should have just finished the lap.”

Leclerc qualified fourth and will start third next to Liam Lawson who was second fastest in Q2 and held provisional pole early in Q3. Lawson, who is not confirmed to have a seat with Racing Bulls or elsewhere next year, had his best qualifying in fifth. His best of the year at both Red Bull and Racing Bulls has been eighth, twice.

Nico Hulkenberg qualified sixth and will start sixth, and Isack Hadjar is the final driver upgraded with Russell’s penalty. Haas’ Oliver Bearman will start eighth, and Fernando Alonso and Alex Albon both made their first Q3 appearances of the year and will start ninth and tenth, respectively.

Thousands gathered at Tenerife's stadium on Saturday to pay their final respects to striker Jesus de Miguel, with captain Enric Gallego fighting back tears to remember a teammate who died aged 30 after suffering a cardiac arrest following a match collision.

De Miguel was taken to intensive care after colliding with Andorra goalkeeper Jesus Owono on Monday. Spanish second-division club Tenerife confirmed his passing on Tuesday morning.

A massive banner displaying his black-and-white photograph and the words "Forever Demi" hung behind a central pitch stage at the Estadio Heliodoro Rodriguez Lopez.

Following a video showing footage of his Tenerife debut, fans chanted: "De Miguel, we love you".

Demi was the nickname used by De Miguel's friends.

"We're still struggling to come to terms with the fact that you won't be with us again," Gallego added.

Addresses by club directors were followed by a religious service during the 90-minute tribute in Santa Cruz de Tenerife.

Matches across all Spanish divisions observed a minute's silence over the weekend in De Miguel's honour.

On Wednesday, Owono spoke out about the “enormous pain” he felt following De Miguel’s death.

“These are the most difficult days of my life,” Owono, an Equatorial Guinea international, said in a statement released by his club Andorra on Wednesday.

“I’m struggling to find the words and finding it very difficult to process anything that has happened.

“I feel enormous pain at the loss of Jesus. I think of him, his family, and all the people who loved him. I know there will be many issues to address, and I will face whatever comes with the utmost respect. But right now, honestly, I can’t think of anything other than Jesus and the pain we are experiencing.

Man City injury news: Enzo Maresca issues Marc Guehi, Josko Gvardiol update after Nottingham Forest defeat

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

By Oliver Thomas | 07 Nov 2026 20:48

Enzo Maresca has provided an update on the fitness of Marc Guehi and Josko Gvardiol following Manchester City’s 3-1 defeat at Nottingham Forest in the Premier League on Saturday.

Both defenders were handed starts in a four-man City backline that conceded twice in the opening 18 minutes courtesy of sloppy mistakes.

Gvardiol was dispossessed from a central position by Jair Cunha who then slotted home Forest’s early opener, before Guehi and Ruben Dias could not prevent powerhouse Liam Delap from barnstorming through on goal and firing the hosts further in front.

Guehi - under fire for recent error-strewn performances - and Gvardiol were then replaced at half time by Nico O'Reilly and Matheus Nunes, and six minutes after the interval, Rayan Cherki rifled the ball into the top corner to give City a glimmer of hope.

However, Forest regained their two-goal cushion thanks to a stunning strike from man-of-the-match Delap, condemning Man City to their third successive Premier League defeat.

Explaining why both Guehi and Gvardiol were withdrawn at half time, Maresca told Sky Sports: "Both injury, both asked for change.

“Marc Guehi [has an] injury and Josko Gvardiol had some issues already during the week, so it was just because they had problems.”

Guehi and Gvardiol were called up to the England and Croatia squads respectively earlier this week ahead of two UEFA Nations League fixtures in November’s international break.

England welcome Croatia to Wembley on November 12 for their penultimate League A Group 3 match, while the Three Lions face Spain and the Checkered Ones take on Czechia in their final game three days later.

However, both Guehi and Gvardiol have now emerged as injury doubts, with an update on their unspecified problems likely to come at the start of next week.

Man City currently have Antoine Semenyo sidelined with a knee injury, with the attacker targeting a return at some stage next month.

O’Reilly, meanwhile, played the second half against Forest but is ‘not 100%’ and has subsequently been left out of Thomas Tuchel’s England squad.

Man City's defensive frailties are a growing concern for Maresca, who highlighted his team's lack of intensity and failure to win duels as major factors behind their disappointing defeat at the City Ground.

“I will speak quite clearly. In the first half when you do not match the intensity and lose all the duels, it is difficult. Then we want to react but it is too late,” Maresca told reporters.

“In these kinds of matches, first of all you need to match the intensity and win the duels. Otherwise it is always complicated.

“In the first half we were not good enough in terms of duels and second balls, we lost all of them.

“Then when we tried to react, we were 2-0 down and in football it is always complicated.

“The first goal we are trying to play and build up. It can happen. It’s the second one, we lost duels in the middle of the pitch, the third one we lost duels in the edge of the box. We don’t need to wait to concede two to react.”

Man City, who are in danger of slipping to third in the Premier League table, are next in action on November 21 at home to Fulham.

SpaceX's December Lockup Is Nearly the Biggest Share Unlock in IPO History. History Says This Is What Happens Next.

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

SpaceX conducted its IPO with a surprisingly low public float.

When Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +8.62%) conducted its initial public offering this summer, it bucked several long-standing trends. One of those trends was to go public by selling at least 10% of shares to the public. Many big tech companies, for example, initially offered 15% to 20% of their shares to the public during their IPOs. SpaceX, however, went public with just 5% of its total outstanding shares available for trade.

There's nothing inherently wrong about offering up only a tiny fraction of a company's outstanding shares for public purchase. But it does create challenges.

The S&P 500 index, for example, typically requires a public float of at least 10%. SpaceX's application to fast-track its inclusion into the index was denied. Low public float can also create additional volatility, as surges in buy or sell volumes can more easily overwhelm available supply. While several other factors were at play, the low public float likely contributed to SpaceX's early stock price volatility.

There is precedent for such a low initial public float. Saudi Aramco raised $25.6 billion with an initial public float of just 1.5%. SpaceX, however, raised a much larger $85.7 billion without upping its public float above 5%.

Very quickly after its IPO, however, SpaceX's public float began to rise. An additional 20% of shares became available when second-quarter earnings were released in August. October's third-quarter results freed up another 28%, with several other unlocking events throughout the adjacent months. In December, another 17% will become available for trade. That essentially frees up all of SpaceX's locked shares, minus the massive chunk of shares CEO Elon Musk will see unlocked next summer -- shares that are unlikely to be sold, and therefore often not included in many lockup percentages.

What will happen in December when an additional 17% of SpaceX's float becomes publicly traded? History has a clear answer.

When SpaceX went public, many analysts were worried that ongoing share unlocks could create selling pressure for the space stock.

In October, SpaceX experienced the largest share unlock of any company in history, freeing up more than $180 billion worth of stock. As analysts predicted, volatility ensued. Early in the month, shares zoomed from $150 to $170, only to shed those gains by the time earnings were announced on Oct. 21. When 1.3 billion shares were unlocked on Oct. 22, heavy supply pressure resulted in a 9% single-day drop. By the end of October, SpaceX's stock price was below its initial IPO price.

The largest unlock period for SpaceX before that was in early August. Roughly $100 billion in shares became available for trading. Shares initially dropped below $110 as the unlock date approached. Afterward, however, shares rallied, ending the month with a 32% gain.

In general, there's no guarantee what will happen before, during, or after a major share unlock. If there were a strong trend, markets -- which are forward-looking -- would quickly price that upward or downward potential into the stock's current valuation. That's especially true given that the market knows exactly when unlock dates will be triggered. Some research suggests that a 1% to 3% drop during an unlocking event is to be expected on average, but that's an incredibly rough estimate for a single security.

In short, investors are likely wise not to trade around SpaceX's lockup period, even if it is one of the largest share unlocks in history. The better move is likely to assess the projected fair value of SpaceX as a business and accumulate shares if selling pressure arises and pushes the company's stock price below your fair value estimate.

Share unlock schedules get a lot of media attention. But in the long term, they mean very little. Even though SpaceX's unlock schedule is historic, expect these events to have minimal impact on the company's long-term success.

Ryan Vanzo has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Space Exploration Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

There is no end in sight for the GPU supply problem

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

AI data centers are gobbling up memory as fast as it's manufactured.

As companies push all their chips into the AI basket, GPU makers are shifting their resources to support the boom. The resulting memory shortage has had dire consequences for the consumer tech industry: Component prices are skyrocketing, supplies are dwindling, and everyday consumers are left holding the bag.

Known colloquially as "chipflation," the rise in GPU prices has vastly outpaced economy-wide inflation levels. According to a report by JP Morgan, by the end of 2026, dynamic random access memory (DRAM) prices will have risen by 400 percent since 2024. The Tom's Hardware chips tracker reflects this increase, finding that Nvidia's most powerful consumer GPU, the GeForce RTX 5090, currently retails for $4,920 above the $1,999 suggested price when it launched in January 2025. Foreign markets are also feeling the squeeze. In Japan, for instance, some gaming GPUs have risen roughly 41 percent above their American counterparts.

The knock-on effecs across the consumer electronics market has been shocking. According to that JP Morgan report, the Consumer Price Index for software and accessories has increased by nearly a quarter over the last two years. A 2026 report by The Economist, meanwhile, found similar rises in the prices of video game consoles and laptops, prompting an uncharacteristic fall in PC shipments. Opportunistic resellers have taken advantage on the resale market, where companies like GameStop market Sony's PlayStation 5 Pro for roughly $500 above MSRP.

Some expect the crisis to extend into the 2030s. Many chip producers already have sold much of their 2027 supplies, with some stating that they are now focusing their sales upon 2028 and beyond.

At its core, the GPU shortage is a memory problem. Of course, peaks and valleys in chip supplies are nothing new. Already, the industry was shifting from DDR4 to DDR5 memory formats, stalling supplies as manufacturers transitioned to new production lines. But the rapid data center buildout has worsened the shortage, necessitating vast stores of high-bandwidth memory (HBM), a specialized form of DRAM with faster connections and more bandwidth. AI developers need HBM chips to both train and run their AI models, so HBM demand far outpaces current market supplies.

Unfortunately, HBM competes for the same wafer capacity as DRAM and other memory products. With resources limited, suppliers have steadily reoriented production toward advanced HBM chips, following not only demand but profit margins, selling HBM for roughly 30 percent more than traditional DRAM. To make matters worse, suppliers are transitioning to a more advanced generation of HBM, namely HBM4 and HBM4E, further limiting supplies as companies transition production.

As a Samsung executive told Reuters in September, HBM could account for nearly 30 percent of total DRAM production in 2027, up from 20 percent the year before. Some expect that percentage to jump to almost 40 percent by 2029. More broadly, AI data centers are projected to consume roughly 70 percent of global DRAM supplies this year.

A highly concentrated market further exacerbates the issue. Today, three companies account for roughly 90 percent of global DRAM production: Micron Technologies, Samsung Electronics, and SK Hynix. All three are expecting long-term shortages despite making major expansions of their production capacity. Moreover, the trio continue to pivot towards datacenters at the expense of consumer GPUs. Micron, for instance, ceased direct-to-consumer sales last year to dedicate resources toward AI production. Cue continued RAM price hikes.

Industry leaders paint a worrisome picture. Micron CEO Sanjay Mehrotra said in a recent earnings call to expect supply to be "much tighter in calendar 2027 and 2028" than in 2026. SK Hynix CEO Kwak Noh-jung echoed this prediction, telling Reuters in July, "we forecast that ‌next year will be the worst year in the industry's history from the supply perspective." SK Hynix's chairman Chey Tae-won, meanwhile, said at a Nvidia event that it will take "at least four to ​five years" to build adequate wafer supply, with production lagging 20 percent behind demand until at least 2030 (via Reuters).

Several major suppliers have already sold most of their production capacity for next year. Micron, for instance, has reportedly sold 75 percent of its 2027 HBM output. According to Mehrotra, via Yahoo Finance, these sales were struck at prices "much higher than 2026 prices." Samsung, meanwhile, has sold almost 80 percent of its 2027 memory output, according to Korean investment firm KB Securities, with roughly 70 percent tied up in five-year contracts.

Some are skeptical that all this will continue. Acer CEO Jason Chen, for instance, told DigiTimes prices could plateau next year, citing a potential influx of cheap Chinese alternatives. Others question whether the AI industry will generate enough growth to warrant memory suppliers' massive buildout. One South Korean publication caused memory stocks to tumble in October 2026 when it quoted experts predicting that a "flood of new supply" in 2029 would transform the memory market "into a red ocean," per a CNBC translation. Nvidia head Jensen Huang, though, waved off the possibility of a supply surplus, telling CNBC, "One of the things that's really great about memory is that you can always use more of it."

→ Baca artikel asli

Deep Purple’s Ian Gillan Shares Encouraging News After Undergoing Surgery

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

"I now have to rest, in order to make a complete recovery," the singer wrote.

Deep Purple’s Ian Gillan is recovering after a medical emergency forced the legendary rock band to postpone its European tour.

On Saturday (Nov. 7), the 81-year-old singer shared a health update with fans, revealing that he had undergone a successful procedure and would need time to rest before making a full recovery.

“I had a procedure yesterday which was successful,” Gillan wrote on Facebook. “I now have to rest, in order to make a complete recovery and ensure that I’m as fit as can be before getting back to work, thanks everybody for your patience and understanding. I really do appreciate it and apologise for all the inconvenience.”

Last weekend, Deep Purple revealed that Gillan had been hospitalized with an unspecified health issue, prompting the postponement of two concerts in Germany. Days later, the band confirmed that the remainder of its scheduled U.K. and European tour would also be postponed due to the singer’s illness.

The cancellations began with the band’s Oct. 31 show at QI Arena in Leipzig and its Nov. 1 date at Sporthalle in Hamburg. Gillan later shared more details about the health scare on Instagram.

“Thank you for all the good wishes and support over the last few days, it means the world. I had a medical problem whilst in Leipzig which required urgent attention at the hospital,” the singer wrote.

“I was hoping that I would get the all clear to hang on until the end of the tour, but there are very serious risks involved in me doing so,” he continued. “As a result, and on the advice of my medical team and management we have made the decision to postpone the rest of the European/UK tour until further notice. I’m very sorry about any inconvenience this may cause, but it’s in the best interest all round.”

Deep Purple has been on the road for its SPLAT! world tour since late summer, with the postponed U.K. and European dates originally set to run through late November. At press time, the band’s Latin American leg was still scheduled to launch Dec. 5 in São Paulo, Brazil, with a final stop in Mexico City on Dec. 19.

Gillan has fronted Deep Purple during multiple stints since first joining the band in 1969. He is the voice behind some of the group’s biggest hits, including its signature 1973 song “Smoke on the Water,” which peaked at No. 4 on the Billboard Hot 100.

‘Save Mass Effect’ movement enlists star-studded VA cast for N7 Day rallying cry, and I’m already getting chills

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Nov. 7, or N7 Day for Mass Effect fans, is usually a time for celebration. This year, the atmosphere is different. The “Save Mass Effect” movement mustered the help a metric ton of the trilogy’s voice actors, asking fans to help overturn the franchise’s dreadful fate.

The video calls on the voices behind iconic characters of the franchise to plead for help saving Mass Effect. The voices of both Shepards, Tali, Jack, Garrus, Grunt, Thane Krios, Legion,

Today is a very special day for Mass Effect fans.We've worked incredibly hard to bring together most of the original Mass Effect trilogy's voice cast to share a very important message with you.We need to #SaveMassEffect. pic.twitter.com/FOp0qp4wBI

“A huge thank you to everyone who has helped make Mass Effect what it is, those featured in this video and many others who aren’t… We are Mass Effect,” a message at the end of the video says. “This is only the beginning. We’re going to save Mass Effect,” it adds.

The campaign also thanked Ash Sroka (Tali), Mark Meer (male Shepard), and Courtenay Taylor (Jack) for reaching out to the rest of the cast. “We couldn’t have brought so many people together without their support,” it said.

The video is the latest move by a campaign that’s been gaining momentum by the day since the dreadful news of BioWare’s closure last month. Yesterday, the campaign parked a mobile billboard outside the Electronic Arts headquarters in California, and that’s after putting up a billboard in Times Square in New York. A Change.org petition asking to save the game has garnered over 300,000 signatures, and similar petition in Canada is asking for help getting the late Edmonton-based studio to “resume business as an independent, Canadian company.”

The valiant push towards saving the franchise seems to borrow inspiration from a similar movement involving the untimely, undeserved demise of Destiny 2. After an overwhelming display of devotion from fans, Bungie’s new leadership promised to restore content that was removed from the game since 2020’s Beyond Light.

BioWare was mercilessly shut down on Oct. 21 after EA changed hands. A lot of material has surfaced since then, including mind-blowing revelations about the next installment in the franchise. A documentary will showcase the studio’s (hopefully not) final days with inside access to its workings.

The campaign has found extraordinary strength, especially throughout social media, which is a testament to the lasting impact BioWare had in the industry. Maybe that’s not how this story ends. After all, “this is only the beginning,” as the campaign said.

REDD — Picking Up Our New Tesla Model Y

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

REDD, the name for our new Tesla Model Y (Standard Range ’26 build, ’27 model), was inspired by the movie of the same name – Retired, Extremely Dangerous Drivers (the wife and I, of course). As mentioned previously, there is nothing wrong with our beloved 2019 Model 3. It is our bodies that are aging and less able to get in and out of our beloved little sporty Tess. So it was with mixed feelings that we arrived at the Tesla delivery centre to pick up the new car.

As always, it was a hive of activity, with deliveries taking place all around us. Quite a contrast to 7 years ago when Tesla was operating out of one cramped delivery spot near the centre of Brisbane, and the handover took less than 3 minutes. This time there were plenty of sales representatives spending their time and making sure we knew what we were doing.

On arrival, we walked past a line consisting of ICE cars being surrendered as trade-ins for an electric. As we left, a car carrier was unloading half a dozen new Model Ys. All in all, a smooth operation. While Majella was getting her phone synced and lots of information from the sales rep, I had a wander around.

The delivery centre is divided in two by a screen. On our side of the screen, I could see at least 60 new cars anxiously awaiting their new owners — like puppies at the pound, I thought. I wasn’t allowed behind the curtain where cars were being detailed and prepped. I commented to our sales rep that there didn’t seem to be many Model 3s. He told me that they had just received a Model Y shipment; the 3s were on their way. Asked about what lay behind the curtain in Tesla wonderland, he said that there were hundreds of cars “back there.”

Majella drove REDD home and I drove Tess. I am having mixed feelings. I get attached to my cars; it was difficult to sell George (my ‘64 Wolseley) and Ruby (my Hyundai Accent). But they were old and tired and their time had come. Tess, at 180,000+ kilometres still feels young. It’s me that’s getting old. The letting go is being made easier because we are selling Tess to our youngest daughter. So, we will still be in contact with the car and watch the milestones as she grows.

As we sipped our celebratory champagne, I mused at the difference between buying the Model Y in 2026 and the Model 3 in 2019. Going from the Hyundai to the Tesla was a massive leap in both design and technology — the screen, the regen braking. Our Tesla was the only one in our suburb, and there was no high-speed charging. Electric vehicles were seen as a “soon to pass” fad on the way to hydrogen cars, and a stupid decision in a country as big as Australia. In 2019, less than one percent of new cars sold had a plug, Tesla was still on a deathwatch. We had taken a risk — would it work out?

It certainly has! Owning Tess and dealing with FUD gave me a cause to champion. I started exploring this new world and writing about it — driving long trips and proving that an EV can do what it has to. CleanTechnica published my ramblings — which have now reached over 1,000 articles. People contacted me and shared their stories of private ownership. CEOs of new startups (like recently introduced Thermal Dawn) gave me interviews and insights. This might sound weird, but moving from the Model 3 to the Model Y is nowhere near as exciting. Yes, I will continue to write, but the motoring world has changed. In Australia, the Model Y has been the highest selling passenger car of all drivetrains for the past few months. In October, it even beat the refreshed Toyota RAV4. My northern Brisbane suburb is full of Tesla Model Ys. The crossover has become common.

Motoring journalists are learning slowly to accept the new kids on the block, and most of their reviews seem to indicate a modicum of tolerance and truth. Of course, we still have vested interests proclaiming that Chinese cars are destroying the Australian car industry. Read: dealerships can’t sell ICE cars and aren’t making enough money. I wonder sometimes what there will be left write about as plugins take more and more of the market, and “real” motoring journalists write the articles I normally write. Relax, I am sure there will be room for all of us for some time. Especially in the USA!

Calls continue for more charging infrastructure, which is fair enough for highway and tourist travel, but seems to totally ignore home charging. “I live in a remote town and there is nowhere to charge my car!” wails one. I could scream if he would hear me: plug it into your house power socket, you moron!

Tess sits in the carport plugged into our home battery. Majella has taken REDD to her gem club. I am sure that instead of their normal lapidary, faceting, and jewellery making, they will be exploring REDD. Four other members of the club have now got Teslas or BYDs. One of them commented that they want a Model Y for Christmas — not sure if his wife will agree.

REDD will also be the guest of honour at our upcoming Coffee, Cake, and EV morning tea in a few days’ time.

Once we have done the roadworthy and registration transfer, Tess will be off to her new home. We will get to see her at family gatherings and when we pick up the grandkidlets from school. Majella is busy buying the accessories — mats, ceiling screen (no, the new heavy tint doesn’t do much in the Australian sun), rim protectors, etc. REDD came with front and back floor mats. She is buying all-weather mats for the frunk and trunk. The dog may even get to sit in the car! Shock! Horror! And we are slowly noticing subtle differences, like the reduction in noise driving on the highway — REDD has better seals and double-glazed windows.

We may have lost some of the apps that came free with Tess (Spotify, Karaoke, connectivity), but we have gained cooling seats and a heated steering wheel. And, of course, Hardware 4, which will give us access to FSD (Supervised) for our long trips. Speaking of longer trips, the range of our Model 3 (about 300 km with some degradation) pales beside the 460 km range of the LFP batteries in REDD. LFP batteries also mean less anxiety about charging to 100% when needed. REDD is longer, wider, and taller, but, thank goodness, still fits in our garage.

Majella is missing Enhanced Autopilot, for which we paid AU$5,000 just a few years ago. The Model Y’s cruise control is not as convenient. Stay tuned for updates as we enter the next leg of our electric journey.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

Arsenal v Hull: Ex-PGMOL Chief Disagrees With Rules Expert Over Penalty Shout

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Arsenal's narrow 1-0 victory over Hull City wasn't without controversy and two experts have determined whether Kai Havertz should have been awarded a penalty.

Martin Odegaard scored the winner for the Gunners, pouncing in the 72nd minute to hand his side all three points and move six points clear of Manchester City heading into November's international break. They came up against a resilient Hull team but Mikel Arteta's attackers were disappointing on the day.

While Odegaard's winner came during open play, he might have already put the hosts in front in the first half from the penalty spot. VAR sent referee Farai Hallam to the pitchside monitor to review an incident between Havertz and Nobel Mendy which led to the German attacker going down in the box.

Hallam explained that although Mendy committed a foul by pulling Havertz to the ground, the decision was a free-kick to Hull because the Arsenal player was in an offside position. VAR's decision to send the referee to the monitor drew scrutiny itself due to the outcome of the review.

BBC Sport's football issues correspondent Dale Johnson has now claimed that Hallam and the officiating team were in correct. He stated that a player in an offside position only becomes active when committing an offside offence.

Johnson clarified that Havertz had not committed such an offence as he didn't challenge for the ball and therefore Mendy's shirt pull takes precedence. He also pointed out that 'the foul took place before any offside offence' but in the end it need not matter as Arteta's men secured all three points.

A similar incident occurred last season between Newcastle United and Bournemouth when Fabian Schar won a penalty after being pulled back from an offside position. On that occasion, VAR's intervention led to a spot-kick being awarded much like.

Arsenal fans were also quick to recall Harry Kane similarly winning a penalty against them back in March 2019. The FA rules stated at that time that 'a player in an offside position is moving towards the ball with the intention of playing the ball and is fouled before playing or attempting to play the ball, or challenging an opponent for the ball, the foul is penalised as it has occurred before the offside offence.'

Former PGMOL chief Keith Hackett of Football Insider interestingly disagreed with Johnson and claimed that Hallam came to the right decision. He did acknowledge that reviewing the incident was unnecessary and should have been prioritised before assessing the foul.

Hackett said: "It is clear that offside is the first offence and therefore the game should restart with an indirect free kick to the defending team. I'm amazed that the offside consideration was not looked at immediately. The correct decision, albeit the wrong way to get to it!"

Arteta didn't seem as angered by the decision as expected given his team prevailed. But he did suggest that he thought it was a penalty when speaking during his post-match press conference, telling reporters that his team 'were one-on-one with the goalkeeper twice today, there’s a penalty there, and so many shots were deflected or blocked.'

3 Best 2026 Books To Read Before the Year Ends

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

We are almost at the end of the year, that magical time when we start to feel like we didn't do nearly enough. So few of us actually get to fulfill our New Year's resolutions — if we have resolutions at all — but there is still time to do a few of them; it's only November, after all. If your resolution was to read a bit more, then you're in luck, because 2026 had a slew of great books, some of which you might not have read yet.

Better late than never, and early November is still a great time for you to pick up one of these 2026 gems and lose yourself in their engaging narratives. Maybe they're a great fantasy adventure, or maybe they're a romantic drama — the point is, these 2026 books are amazing, and you still have time to start and finish them before the ball drops on December 31.

Let's begin with a cute romantasy book, because who doesn't love romantasy? Sea of Charms is the third standalone entry in the Spellshop series and tells a classic high-seas adventure with plenty of romance and fantasy to boot. The plot follows Marin, a supplier runner who journeys from island to island to deliver goods alongside her crew. On the island of Alyssium, Marin meets Dax, a stubborn composer whom she convinces to pose as her boyfriend in the upcoming End-of-Harvest festival.

Sea of Charms blends a typical romcom plot with a thrilling adventure at sea, and the result is one of the most singular and enchanting books of 2026. Every cozy trope that romance fans love is alive and well here: fake dating? Check! Found family? Check! Self-discovery amid hardship? Check! What else can you ask for? Author Sarah Beth Durst's writing is witty, atmospheric, and utterly irresistible, making it impossible not to fall for Marin and Dax. Mainly, her immersive prose will make it impossible to put Sea of Charms down, and at 384 pages, it's a perfect choice to finish before Christmas Day. However, perhaps there's no better time to read this than in the week between Christmas and New Year.

Now for something a lot more serious and relevant. The Rise and Fall of the Artificial State is a non-fiction book about, you guessed it, AI. Written by Harvard historian Jill Lepore, the book deals with heavy issues concerning how artificial intelligence and corporations are messing with the democratic process. Lepore tracks the evolution of systems purportedly designed to help democracy, including ID numbers and consensus techniques, and describes how they have become tools for mass surveillance and categorization.

In a world where AI is such a hot topic of discussion, books like The Rise and Fall of the Artificial State become all the more relevant and important. Interestingly, Lepore tracks how many of sci-fi's most daring ideas, first presented in books and movies, have been developed in real life, ultimately amounting to the situation we find ourselves in right now. The author also pulls no punches when it comes to condemning oligarchs and tech moguls, who keep pushing for invasive technologies aimed at slowly eroding democracy. Non-fiction books can be just as compelling as any work of narrative fiction, and this gem proves it; chances are there is no 2026 book more passionate or urgent than this one.

Finally, we have a wonderfully original book that updates F. Scott Fitzgerald's The Great Gatsby for the new millennium. Last Night in Brooklyn is set in 2007, shortly before the 2008 financial crisis that would shape much of the early 2010s lifestyle. The protagonist is Alicia Canales Forten, a 26-year-old whose conventional life is upended by her mysterious and alluring neighbor, a designer known as La Garza.

A genderbent version of The Great Gatsby might not be particularly interesting in and of itself, but author Xochitl Gonzalez opts for a modern and insightful approach. Here, she deals with classic themes in line with the setting — indeed, everything from economic uncertainty to class and racial struggles can be found here. However, Gonzalez is far more perceptive than the average writer, particularly concerning issues of ambition and what it means to exist in a world driven by appearance and money. Moreover, the book is incredibly atmospheric, with the author painting a vibrant, explosive vision of Brooklyn that feels powerful, irresistibly immersive, and ultimately unforgettable.

The No. 1 Horror Movie of 2026 Is Officially Leaving Peacock

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Unless you have a One Wish Willow, you'll want to get your final watch in

Not many directors can say their debut film was a blockbuster hit, let alone a cultural phenomenon. But newcomer Curry Barker, who wrote and directed Obsession, the No. 1 horror movie of 2026, can say he's accomplished both. The film takes a look at the dangers of getting exactly what you wish for when Bear (Michael Johnston) makes a wish that his friend Nikki Freeman (Inde Navarrette) would love him more than anyone else in the world. Despite being made on only a $750,000 budget, the film dominated the box office when it hit theaters, earning $17.2 million in its first week and a staggering $23.9 million in its second week.

To date, Obsession remains the tenth most profitable film of 2026 at the worldwide box office according to data from Box Office Mojo, earning a gross total of $523 million during its theatrical run. It's no surprise that when Obsession moved to streaming, it continued finding success on Peacock, but going into November, the end of Obsession's victory lap is officially drawing near.

Obsession is leaving Peacock on Nov. 17, 2026, the same date that the film is expected to launch on Netflix in the United States. The film first hit Peacock on July 17, 2026, and drew in 456 million minutes streamed in just its first two days on the service, according to data from Nielsen. It went on to spend more than 150 days on JustWatch’s U.S. streaming Top 10 list, further cementing its domination in the industry.

Obsession was more than just a blockbuster and streaming hit. It became a cultural phenomenon in the months that followed its release, with fans drawn into conversations online about the film's themes surrounding bodily autonomy and consent. A Nikki Freeman actor drew thousands of visitors to California's Halloween Horror Nights this fall, including Inde Navarette herself, and Obsession reached as far as Fortnite, where a Nikki Freeman skin was released for the game in November 2026 (complete with a "No, No, No..." emote).

Even Barker, who first built a following for himself making sketches and short films on YouTube, didn't expect the success that came with Obsession. In a June 2026 interview with the Associated Press, he said, "“That’s when you start to feel this impostor syndrome of like: What? It’s not that good. All I see when I watch ‘Obsession’ is the problems.”

The film follows Bear (Michael Johnston), a lonely, awkward man with strong, unrequited feelings for his friend Nikki Freeman (Inde Navarrette). After failing to confess his feelings, Bear stumbles upon a One Wish Willow in a novelty shop. The instructions seem simple enough: make a wish, break the willow stick, and watch your deepest desires come true, but when Bear wishes for Nikki to love him more than anyone else in the entire world, his dream quickly turns into a nightmare. The film has received plenty of praise from critics and audience members, including Polygon writer Jesse Hassenger, who wrote, "Navarrette gets plenty of time to believably and charmingly establish who Nikki is before the wish takes her true self away. Once that happens, she does bravura work as this imitation version, regularly flying off the handle with panicked eagerness or cackling mania, creating an unusual combination of fear and pity — a true movie monster crossing possession with a Bride of Frankenstein figure."

It's worth noting that Obsession's move to Netflix this month comes as awards season nears, and it could very well be a strategic decision to keep the film in the spotlight. Focus Features, the production company behind Obsession, is definitely pushing for consideration in the forthcoming Academy Awards. The company's website suggests Obsession for Best Picture, Inde Navarrette for Best Actress, and Curry Barker for Best Original Screenplay, among other categories.

Obsession is about to get another boom in viewership

Beauty Marks: The Best Beauty Looks of the Week

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Welcome back to Beauty Marks: Vogue’s weekly edition of the best moments in celebrity beauty, from Vogue editors’ IG feeds, and all the glam of the fashion and pop culture landscapes. Each week, we curate the nail art to pin for your next nail appointment, new recruits to ‘Team Bob’, and major red carpet moments from the week’s most glamorous affairs. As always, it’s as much about celebrity beauty as it is about the makeup artists, hairstylists, and nail artists, as well as the creators crafting the trends you’re about to see everywhere.

This week in beauty was a study in contrasts: sculptural hair met soft-focus makeup, while barely-there lips shared the spotlight with high-impact eyes. Sienna Miller made a romantic case for the blurred lip, Emily Blunt embraced a wintry shade of blonde, and Kim Petras dialed up the drama with graphic liner and ice-blonde bangs. Elsewhere, Kate Young turned a diamond into the ultimate hair accessory, Kris Jenner revisited the razor-sharp bob, and Lily Allen served up a masterclass in hair transformations. From elaborate nail art to gravity-defying chignons, there was plenty to look twice at.

Scroll through the week’s best of celebrity beauty and pop culture’s glam below, and head to the app to vote for your favorite.

Jennifer Lopez was in full bombshell mode, with glossy, caramel-swirled waves by Chris Appleton, bronzed, softly sculpted makeup and a pillowy nude lip by Ernesto Casillas, and a gleaming posy pink and almond-shaped manicure courtesy of Tom Bachik.

Emily Blunt embraces the winter blonde: her icy, champagne-toned lengths fashioned into an immaculate sculptural updo by Renato Campora. A wash of rosy blush, softly defined eyes, and a precise red lip by Jenn Streicher complemented the cool-toned elegance, with pale glossy nails by Julie K.

Natanya went full celestial showgirl for her London performance, with individually placed silver Swarovski crystals tracing razor-sharp jet-black wings, icy metallic inner corners, and cherub-pink blush by Georgia Hope. Essa sculpted her glossy reddish brunette hair into a sweeping side part, with a delicate, forehead-framing kiss curl.

Lily Allen’s West End Girl tour has been a veritable hair marathon, with six distinctly different looks dreamed up by Chad Maxwell. Our favorite? A tousled, rock-chick half-updo with choppy bangs, teased volume, and loose, face-framing tendrils. Smoky ’60s eyes and a muted rose lip by Lilly Keys completed the look.

A diamond-studded twist on the classic chignon: At the CFDA Awards, Kate Young wore her platinum blonde hair slicked into a sculptural spiral bun by Jacob Rozenberg, with a sparkling, mesmerizing black-and-diamond Taffin jewel nestled at its center. This season, it doesn’t get much chicer than this piece of hair jewelry.

Nail artist Mei Kawajiri turned a set of extra-long, square-tipped acrylics into a miniature cabinet of curiosities. Scribbled illustrations, pastel washes, and translucent 3D charms—including a tiny beer mug, a white cross, and a miniature tart—made each nail its own surreal little artwork.

Once known for her signature pixie, birthday girl Kris Jenner has been enjoying the sleek, jet-black micro-bob for some time now; here, tucked behind the ears and flicked out at the ends by Justine Marjan. Ash K Holm went for fluttery lashes, softly bronzed cheeks, and a pink-nude lip, with the full, feathery brows taking center stage.

Sienna Miller was all soft-focus romance at the Madden premiere. Nina Park gave her a beautifully blurred rose-pink lip, flushed cheeks, and delicately smudged eyes, while Jenny Cho swept her honey-blonde waves into a loose, half-up style, with wispy tendrils just touching her clavicle.

Kim Petras served icy pop princess for a rave at False Idols, with waist-skimming platinum lengths and wispy, brow-grazing bangs by James Lear. Helen Rose sharpened her baby-blue eyes with graphic black wings, spiky lashes, frosted inner corners, and a glossy, overlined pink pout.

A lesson in hair origami: George Curran crafted Victoria Turner’s buttery blonde lengths into an intricate, double-tiered chignon, with two sculptural loops stacked at the nape and a single strand left loose around the face. Hair by way of hero products from Kérastase, with makeup by Amir.

‘This goal shows everyone Rodrigo is here’ – Muniz eager to add to Fulham tally

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Rodrigo Muniz is eager to score more goals and play more minutes for Fulham after netting against Newcastle United on Saturday.

The Cottagers held the Magpies to a 1-1 draw at Craven Cottage, with Muniz cancelling out Jacob Murphy's stunner for the visitors.

There were just 179 seconds between Murphy's opening goal for Newcastle in the 80th minute and Muniz equalising for Fulham in the 83rd minute.

Fulham have drawn a Premier League home game against Newcastle for the first time in 19 meetings, their first league draw in London since March 1984, in the second tier.

Muniz, meanwhile, scored his first league goal of the season, already matching his total from 2025-26, and the Brazilian is keen to push on.

"Last game was really difficult against Aston Villa, then this week we trained hard, we knew this would be a tough game," Muniz told Premier League Studios.

"Newcastle have some really good players so happy to score to help my team-mates.

"I train hard every week to get a chance to play and to score. I need to be ready. In the first half, I said to Kevin 'I need to be ready, I need to warm up well and when I come on I need to do everything'.

"Last season was really difficult for me. This goal was really important for me. Last season, I scored only one goal, not good. I want to play more and this goal shows everyone Rodrigo is here."

Top support at the Cottage.  https://t.co/Tec1GBVAjS

— Fulham Football Club (@FulhamFC) November 7, 2026

Newcastle have avoided defeat in three consecutive Premier League games in London (W2 D1) for the first time since a run of eight games between October 2022 and October 2023 (W4 D4).

Murphy scored his first Premier League goal in 19 games, his first ever left-footed goal in the competition.

Eight of Murphy's last nine Premier League goals for Newcastle have either put his side ahead (seven) or equalised (one), and the winger was delighted with the Magpies' overall performance.

"We're unhappy not to come away with three points. But we're delighted with the overall performance," Murphy told Premier League Studios.

"We've had a tough week and we bounced back. That was a collective performance. Everyone stood up and was counted for.

"We've always been a committed group and I think we showed that. We didn't let Fulham play the style they wanted, we made it uncomfortable for them and, on another day, we were 2-0 up in the first half.

"Disappointed we conceded after the goal, but it's a point in the right direction."

Inter test will reveal Como’s true level, says Fabregas

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Como have been the talk of European football this week after putting nine goals past Lens, but Cesc Fabregas feels Sunday's clash with Inter will say more about their level.

On Wednesday, Fabregas saw his team crush Lens 9-1 in the Champions League, tying the largest margin of victory in the competition's history (also Liverpool 8-0 Besiktas in 2007 and Real Madrid 8-0 Malmo in 2015).

Como also routed Manchester United 4-1 in their previous European outing, while their domestic form has been good, too.

With 23 points on the board after 10 games in Serie A, they know a victory over the Nerazzurri this weekend would see them leapfrog the Scudetto holders in the table.

Going into matchday 11, the Opta supercomputer assigned Como a 21% chance of a stunning title win, making them second-favourites behind Inter (44.3%).

But Fabregas insists Christian Chivu's side are still the benchmark in Serie A, even if they suffered a disappointing derby defeat to AC Milan last weekend.

"It feels like every week you ask me about a new test of our maturity," Fabregas told reporters.

"We knew this season would be special for us, there is so much motivation, but we know our path.

"This is an important match, we are coming off a good run, but it is only a test to see where we can go. Inter remain the strongest team in Italy – we will see our level later.

"I never stop learning from the best. The victory over Lens can happen once – if we're lucky – in our lives.

"Inter are the reigning champions of Italy, they won the Coppa Italia, they played in two Champions League finals. But who knows? Tomorrow, we might spring a surprise with the perfect performance."

Nine goals against RC Lens, a new Champions League away-win record and a night that will stay with Como 1907.  #Como1907 #UCL #RCLComo https://t.co/XBeWAU7iSn

Fabregas also highlighted Arsenal boss Mikel Arteta as an inspiration for what he is attempting to build at Como, who have risen rapidly since the former midfielder took the reins in Serie B in 2023.

"Arteta sets a great example for all of us, with what he is building at Arsenal," Fabregas said.

"The patience shown by the club, even when there were moments of tension and he was eighth in the table, that is what we need, too. I see him as inspiration for what we want to do."

Inter did get back to winning ways in Europe this week, beating Feyenoord 3-0, though they will again be without talismanic captain Lautaro Martinez on Sunday, with the Argentine still recovering from a hamstring injury.

After scoring in three consecutive Serie A games, Thuram has failed to find the net in his last two – he has not had a longer drought since a five-match streak in February and March.

The Inter forward has scored four goals in three league matches against Como, though, only bettering this tally against Parma (five goals in five games) and Torino (seven goals in five games).

Douvikas scored a hat-trick in Como's midweek thrashing of Lens, but Inter are – along with Cagliari and AC Milan, one of three teams he has faced three times in Serie A without either or assisting.

With a goal here, he would become the 10th player to reach double figures for both home and away goals in the last three Serie A seasons, after team-mate Nico Paz and Inter duo Thuram and Lautaro Martinez, the latter of whom is out injured.

Inter have won their last 11 Serie A matches against Como, their longest winning run against a single opponent in the competition's history.

Additionally, the Nerazzurri are the side against whom Como have suffered the most overall defeats in the top flight (21).

Same fixture, let’s rewind https://t.co/t4RIINElnl

Chivu's side will be desperate to bounce back following their derby defeat last weekend, which came off the back of a 19-game unbeaten run (W13 D6) – they have not lost back-to-back Serie A games since August and September 2025 (versus Udinese and Juventus).

Inter have scored 17 goals in their first five home Serie A games this season, winning all of those matches. In the last 60 years, they have scored more than 17 goals in their first six home league games on only two occasions, netting 18 in both 1999-00 and 2021-22.

But Como should not be expected to roll over, and with both teams committed to playing on the front foot, there should be fireworks.

Inter and Como have each recorded 81 high turnovers in Serie A this season, fewer only than Roma's 96. They also rank first and second in the division for 10+ pass sequences (Como 211, Inter 188), and build-up attacks (Como 57, Inter 50).

US sanctions hit 41 companies accused of keeping Prince Group’s scam network running

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Treasury says the network concealed ownership and sustained Prince Group operations, while US rules require covered assets to be blocked.

The US sanctioned four people and 41 entities on November 5, 2026, targeting a corporate network that Treasury says sustained Prince Group's operations after earlier sanctions. The designations subject covered assets to US blocking and reporting requirements, with restrictions also reaching companies that meet OFAC's ownership threshold.

The Office of Foreign Assets Control (OFAC) said the network helped Prince Group, which it has designated a transnational criminal organization, manage offshore structures, conceal beneficial ownership and maintain commercial activities. It links the group's affiliates to digital asset investment scams, romance scams and other online fraud, with many operations relying on trafficked and coerced workers in Southeast Asian compounds.

The action follows earlier Prince Group sanctions on October 14, 2025 and June 23, 2026. The newly sanctioned individuals are Qiu Yong, Zhao Mengyao, He Yujing and Visal Pang, according to the OFAC designation record.

One chain runs from Qiu Yong through Sun Speed Holdings Limited, registered in the British Virgin Islands, to Hong Kong money lender Premium Financial Limited. Treasury says Qiu ultimately owns the lender and controls other BVI and Hong Kong companies on Prince Group's behalf.

Zhao serves as Qiu's personal assistant and as secretary and co-director of several Hong Kong companies he ultimately controls, Treasury said.

Treasury traces Qiu Yong's ownership through an offshore company to a Hong Kong lender. Explore how US blocking obligations apply.

Designated property in the United States or US persons' possession or control is blocked and reportable to OFAC.

The standing 50 Percent Rule covers direct or indirect ownership, individually or in aggregate, by one or more blocked persons.

The named target and the ownership path both matter when assessing sanctions exposure.

The ownership diagram is a selected path within the larger network. Transaction prohibitions retain general or specific OFAC license and exemption qualifications and their stated US jurisdictional scope.

Treasury also targeted He Yujing, the wife of Prince Group executive Chen Bo, and Pang. Treasury says Chen transferred ownership of a Tokyo mansion to He after the October 2025 sanctions action. It says Pang controls 11 Cambodian companies, several co-directed with Chen, whose own US designation came on June 23, 2026.

The November action also names companies linked to previously sanctioned Prince Group executives Dai An and Thet Li. Treasury says Singapore-based Greenbay Multi Media is wholly owned by Thet Li.

Designated persons' property and interests in property in the United States, or in US persons' possession or control, are blocked and must be reported to OFAC.

The standing ownership rule also blocks entities owned 50% or more, directly or indirectly and individually or in the aggregate, by one or more blocked persons. That makes the ownership chain relevant alongside the names on the sanctions list.

Unless a general or specific OFAC license authorizes a transaction, or an exemption applies, transactions involving designated or otherwise blocked property are generally prohibited for US persons or within or transiting the United States. Treasury says violations can bring civil or criminal penalties, with civil penalties possible on a strict liability basis.

For counterparties assessing exposure, OFAC's guidance calls for ownership due diligence on entities involved in transactions and account relationships.

Liverpool are reportedly considering a move for Brighton goalkeeper Bart Verbruggen as they prepare for Alisson Becker’s potential departure next summer.

According to a report from Football Insider, the Reds are among the clubs closely monitoring the 24-year-old Dutch international, who has established himself as one of the Premier League’s most impressive goalkeepers.

Liverpool are keen to strengthen their goalkeeping department, and Verbruggen could be an excellent long-term addition. The Brighton star has registered 11 clean sheets in 14 appearances across all competitions this season, attracting attention from several elite European clubs.

The Reds are understood to have admired the Netherlands international for some time, and they could now explore bringing him to Anfield.

Alisson’s uncertain future has made the situation increasingly important for Liverpool. The Brazilian’s contract expires at the end of the season, and the club are reportedly preparing for the possibility of losing their experienced number one.

The 34-year-old has also endured some disappointing moments this campaign, raising further questions about his long-term future. Liverpool have therefore started assessing potential alternatives, with Verbruggen emerging as an attractive candidate.

However, Liverpool will face considerable competition for his signature. Manchester United and Chelsea have also been credited with an interest in the Brighton goalkeeper, while Bayern Munich are keeping tabs on his situation.

Real Madrid are another potential destination, and previous reports suggest Verbruggen could find it difficult to reject an opportunity to join the Spanish giants.

Nevertheless, Liverpool could attempt to position themselves favourably ahead of next summer’s transfer window.

At just 24, Verbruggen has plenty of room for improvement and already has valuable Premier League experience. His performances suggest he could become a reliable successor to Alisson.

It remains to be seen whether Liverpool will make a formal approach, but he could be an exciting addition to their squad.

West Ham Go Full Circle as Blackburn Expose Familiar Problems

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

West Ham’s 2-1 defeat against Blackburn Rovers was a particularly frustrating result because, for large periods of the game, Scott Parker’s side appeared to be the better team. We started brightly, enjoyed plenty of possession and created opportunities, yet somehow managed to leave Ewood Park with nothing.

What concerns me most isn’t necessarily the result itself, although that’s bad enough. It’s the manner in which West Ham gradually deteriorated as the afternoon progressed. Even after Joël Piroe pulled a goal back late on, I thought we looked increasingly disorganised and short of confidence.

And that’s perhaps the strangest aspect of Parker’s first two matches. We started against Cardiff looking completely devoid of confidence, rediscovered it during that extraordinary second-half comeback, began confidently against Blackburn and somehow finished looking just as nervous as we had before.

I was actually quite encouraged by the opening stages. West Ham pressed high, moved the ball around reasonably well and looked determined to take the game to Blackburn. Max Kilman was particularly proactive, stepping out of defence to offer an additional passing option, while Divine Mukasa and Arne Engels were regularly involved in midfield.

Down the left, Ezra Mayers and Edvin Austbø developed a promising partnership. Mayers was willing to get forward and Austbø looked lively, producing one lovely piece of skill to beat two Blackburn players before delivering a dangerous cross.

Blackburn 2-1 West Ham: Austbo was one of the Hammers better players

However, there was a problem developing on that side of the pitch. Blackburn repeatedly managed to isolate Mayers against two opponents, and while the youngster was doing some good things offensively, he wasn’t receiving enough protection defensively.

Joël Piroe was also struggling to make the most of promising situations. On one occasion, he found himself with only a single defender to beat but hesitated for so long that three opponents eventually surrounded him. West Ham were having plenty of the ball, but Blackburn were defending deeply and making it difficult to create clear opportunities.

The second half initially offered more encouragement. Mavropanos headed narrowly wide from an Engels corner, Piroe had an effort saved and then Austbø produced an excellent cross which Bowen headed against the post. At that stage, West Ham were piling on the pressure and Blackburn were struggling to get out of their own half.

Then came the sucker punch. In the 54th minute, Blackburn broke down their right, with Mayers unable to stop the delivery into the penalty area. Andri Guðjohnsen converted, but I thought several West Ham players could have done better.

Austbø might have provided more protection for Mayers, Mavropanos could have defended the situation better and I certainly felt Mads Hermansen should have been more commanding. Whether he caught the cross, punched it or simply got something on the ball, I thought the goalkeeper had an opportunity to intervene.

It was a horrible goal to concede, particularly given how much of the attacking West Ham had been doing. Parker responded by introducing Manor Solomon for Austbø, a substitution I found surprising. The Norwegian had been one of our more involved attacking players, while Solomon subsequently struggled to make any impression.

Mohamadou Kanté was then replaced by Taty Castellanos, but West Ham were finding it increasingly difficult to establish the same attacking rhythm. Blackburn’s second goal arrived in the 71st minute, with Afolayan producing an excellent finish.

Kilman had gone down following contact to his face, but too many West Ham players appeared more interested in appealing for a foul than dealing with the danger. Engels, in particular, could have done more to close down the shot. Whatever the circumstances, you cannot simply stop defending because you believe the referee should have awarded a free kick.

At 2-0, the game appeared to be slipping away, but West Ham eventually found a route back through something remarkably straightforward. Castellanos got down the right-hand side and delivered a dangerous ball into the penalty area. Piroe reacted quickly and fired high into the roof of the net in the 82nd minute.

It was an excellent finish from a striker who, in truth, hadn’t been having a particularly good afternoon. But sometimes football really is that simple. Get the ball wide, get down the line and deliver something dangerous into the box. Give your centre-forward an opportunity to attack the ball.

Suddenly it was Blackburn 2-1 West Ham, with enough time remaining to rescue a point. Unfortunately, the expected late onslaught never really materialised. In fact, I thought West Ham became progressively worse.

Mavropanos endured a dreadful few minutes, fortunate not to concede a penalty before slipping and gifting Blackburn a counter-attack. He then slipped again while attempting to recover, while Souček subsequently played a very poor pass into Lewis Orford inside our own penalty area, creating another potentially disastrous situation.

West Ham looked increasingly nervous, and the defensive organisation seemed to disappear. There were still opportunities at both ends in stoppage time, with Todd Cantwell shooting straight at Hermansen when Blackburn might have made it 3-1.

Souček then headed directly at the Blackburn goalkeeper following an excellent chipped delivery from Bowen. It was precisely the sort of position from which we’ve seen Souček score numerous times, but once again West Ham had failed to make the most of a promising opportunity.

I keep coming back to the confidence issue because I think it explains an awful lot about what we’ve witnessed over these two matches. Against Cardiff, West Ham were absolutely dreadful during the opening 45 minutes, before producing a transformation after half-time that was almost impossible to believe.

Suddenly the players looked confident, aggressive and capable of playing some very good football. That improvement appeared to carry into the opening stages at Ewood Park, where we pressed Blackburn, moved the ball with purpose and looked considerably more comfortable in possession.

Yet by the closing stages, the confidence had completely evaporated. It was almost as though we’d gone full circle in the space of two matches, ending up precisely where we started. That is particularly disappointing because I still believe this West Ham squad possesses considerably more ability than recent performances suggest.

There were some positives. Mukasa showed invention, Austbø looked dangerous, Kilman had a decent game and Piroe took his goal extremely well. But the defensive mistakes, poor decision-making and increasingly nervous football were all too familiar.

I don’t think Scott Parker can be held responsible for everything we’ve witnessed this week. He’s barely had any meaningful time on the training pitch, and the international break offers him an opportunity to start properly implementing his ideas.

However, the evidence of these two games suggests that restoring confidence isn’t simply a matter of delivering one rousing team talk. The psychological side of West Ham’s problems may be every bit as important as the tactical side.

The frustrating thing is that we’ve already seen how much better these players can perform when they believe in themselves. Against Blackburn, we watched that belief gradually disappear again.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live. He previously served as an official video and content creator for West Ham United FC during the club's tenure at Upton Park and featured in the official documentary commemorating the historic final game at the Boleyn Ground.
